#2936. 2 of the Best Dividend Stocks to Buy With $5,000 in November

These two dividend stocks offer reliable raises year after year.

Investors with different goals and preferences will gravitate toward different companies, even if they have the same investment strategy. That's why it's so hard to come up with "the best" of any category of stocks. But we can still give it a try. Consider the following two companies: Johnson & Johnson (JNJ -1.36%) and Walmart (WMT -0.69%). Both are longtime market leaders with excellent dividend programs and are arguably among the top income stocks, given their impressive track records.

For those with $5,000 to spare (make sure it isn't set aside for emergencies), Johnson & Johnson and Walmart are two of the first dividend stocks I'd recommend buying this month. Here is why.

Johnson & Johnson has shown its resilience this year. Despite macroeconomic problems, a major U.S. patent cliff it experienced last year, and several of its medicines being targeted by government-led drug price negotiations, the healthcare giant has performed well. Third-quarter revenue increased by 5.9% year over year to $25.4 billion.

Johnson & Johnson's adjusted earnings per share declined 7.5% year over year to $2.59, primarily due to acquisition-related expenses. Overall, the business is healthy, and Johnson & Johnson once again increased its guidance for the full fiscal year 2026. The company now expects operational sales of $100.7 billion at the midpoint, representing a 6.9% year-over-year increase, versus midpoint sales and year-over-year growth of $100.6 billion and 6.8% year-over-year for its previous guidance.

Johnson & Johnson has also made meaningful clinical and regulatory progress this year, which should help the company boost sales and navigate patent cliffs over the medium term. In March, the company earned approval for Icotyde, a medicine for plaque psoriasis. It has had an impressive commercial ramp-up so far. As management noted, more than 20,000 patients have initiated treatment with Icotyde so far.

The company believes this drug can generate more than $5 billion in peak annual sales. And it boasts several other brand-new approvals or pipeline candidates that it believes can approach or even exceed the $5 billion annual sales milestone. We haven't even mentioned Johnson & Johnson's medtech business, where it has also made progress this year.

The company has earned clearance for the Ottava system, a robotic-assisted surgery device that will help it tap into an underpenetrated market. Johnson & Johnson looks unstoppable right now, and the company could continue delivering excellent returns for a while, especially once we factor in the dividend, which investors should seriously consider reinvesting.

Johnson & Johnson is a Dividend King, a company with a minimum of 50 consecutive annual dividend increases. Investors can get 17 shares of Johnson & Johnson with $5,000 right now. That'd be money well spent.

Walmart has also performed well this year, despite economic headwinds that have weighed on much of the retail industry. In the second quarter of its fiscal year 2027, which ended July 31, the company's revenue increased by 5.9% year over year to $187.9 billion. Adjusted EPS rose 19.1% year over year to $0.81. Importantly, membership revenue, e-commerce sales, and advertising revenue all grew much faster than retail sales.

These are three crucial, higher-margin businesses that should help lift the company's profits over the long run. Here's another important point to consider. While many legacy retailers went out of business -- or are currently struggling to stay in business -- because they failed to capitalize on the shift toward e-commerce, Walmart made no such mistake.

It currently stands as the second largest e-commerce player in the U.S. That highlights the company's ability to capitalize on new technologies, and it is doing the same with artificial intelligence (AI). Walmart is leveraging AI across much of its business, including in optimizing inventory management and improving supply chain efficiency. It is also using AI to personalize customers' shopping experiences, which can lead to higher sales volume.

Walmart's AI efforts could make a meaningful impact over the long run. Meanwhile, the company still has many of the same strengths that made it so successful. It is one of the largest retailers in the U.S., with a vast network of locations -- 90% of the U.S. population lives within 10 miles of one of the company's stores. Further, Walmart leverages its large retail footprint to negotiate attractive prices and discounts with wholesalers and passes those cost savings onto customers. The result: Walmart tends to offer lower prices for items.

These are important competitive advantages that could enable the company to perform well over the long run, while continuously raising its dividends. Walmart is also a Dividend King -- it has raised its payouts for 53 consecutive years. Investors can buy 38 shares of the company for $5,000 right now. For long-term dividend-seeking investors, that might be a great move.

#2921. Johnson & Johnson vs. Vertex Pharmaceuticals: Which Healthcare Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Johnson & Johnson's diversified MedTech and pharma mix faces off against Vertex's cystic fibrosis dominance, but their valuation gap tells a story worth weighing.

Healthcare investors often choose between established stability and high-growth innovation. Determining whether Johnson & Johnson (JNJ -1.36%) or Vertex Pharmaceuticals (VRTX -0.67%) is the superior choice requires examining their financial health and long-term strategies.

Johnson & Johnson is a diversified giant pivoting toward its most profitable medical technology and innovative medicine segments. Vertex Pharmaceuticals is a specialized biotechnology leader that is successfully diversifying its pipeline beyond its core treatments. Both companies provide unique advantages for investors looking toward 2027.

As a giant among healthcare stocks, the company focuses on its Innovative Medicine and MedTech segments to drive global growth. It distributes medications and devices to retailers, wholesalers, and hospitals, with a strategy centered on oncology, immunology, and neuroscience. Notable collaborations include an exclusive license for Darzalex from Genmab A/S (GMAB +0.76%) and a partnership with AbbVie (ABBV -0.59%) to co-commercialize the cancer drug Imbruvica.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 27, 2026, revenue reached about $99.3 billion, representing growth of roughly 7.8% compared to the prior period. During this same time frame, it reported net income of approximately $16.1 billion. This performance highlights the company's ability to maintain substantial revenue while navigating the complexities of the global medical marketplace.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the company maintains a debt-to-equity ratio of approximately 0.6x, which measures the balance between its total debt and shareholder equity. Its current ratio stands at nearly 1.0x, a metric that indicates whether a firm can cover its short-term obligations with its current assets. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 28, 2025, free cash flow was around $19.7 billion, providing ample capital for research and dividends.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals dominates the market for cystic fibrosis treatments and is aggressively expanding into gene-editing and acute pain therapies. It primarily sells its products to specialty pharmacies and distributors that provide access to patients and healthcare providers worldwide. The company is also collaborating with CRISPR Therapeutics (CRSP -0.55%) to develop innovative therapies for serious blood disorders.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached about $13.0 billion, which indicates a growth rate of roughly 10.5% over the previous twelve-month period. The company also reported net income of approximately $4.0 billion during this time. This steady growth reflects the strong demand for its specialized therapies and its successful efforts to broaden its clinical pipeline.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, Vertex carries a debt-to-equity ratio of approximately 0.1x, reflecting a conservative use of borrowed funds. Its current ratio is roughly 2.9x, suggesting a strong ability to meet short-term financial commitments. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was around $3.2 billion, which supports its ongoing investment in research and development.

Johnson & Johnson faces significant legal risks, particularly regarding product liability litigation for its talc-containing products. The company also deals with challenges to its intellectual property rights, as key products like Stelara face potential competition from biosimilars. Additionally, drug pricing reforms in the U.S. and the planned separation of its Orthopedics business introduce operational and strategic uncertainty.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals carries risks related to its high revenue concentration, as nearly all of its sales come from its cystic fibrosis franchise. The competitive landscape for biotechnology remains intense, and the company must navigate pricing pressures from government and commercial payors. Furthermore, the development of cell and gene therapies involves high regulatory uncertainty and significant financial investment in specialized manufacturing.

Johnson & Johnson currently trades at a more modest valuation than Vertex Pharmaceuticals based on its lower multiples for both future earnings and sales.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

The Forward P/E ratio compares a company's current stock price to its earnings estimates for the next twelve months. The P/S ratio measures the market value of the company relative to its sales over the past twelve months.

Johnson & Johnson is an overlooked behemoth compared with faster-growing biotech companies. The business has 28 products and platforms that generate more than $1 billion in annual revenue. Its pharmaceuticals arm, Innovative Medicine, has received two FDA approvals this year and has two more approved drug product launches in the E.U. and the U.S. The Innovative Medicine division accounts for 65% of JNJ's annual revenue and appears to have a robust pipeline beyond the pending products. MedTech, its other arm, sells a wide range of healthcare products, including wound care, surgical instruments and implants, hip and knee replacements, and spinal implants. The recent buy of Atraverse Medical bolsters that business. Revenue for Johnson & Johnson is expected to rise to $101 billion in the current fiscal year. However, Wall Street anticipates a $10 billion drop in net income, to around $16.6 billion.

Vertex Pharmaceuticals continues to benefit from its focus on cystic fibrosis (CF). It recently launched Alyftrek, a once-daily, next-in-class, triple-combination CF regimen. The treatment is seen encouragingly by investors. Alyftrek marks a notable improvement to the twice-daily dosage of Vertex's other CF products and was approved in Australia, New Zealand, Switzerland, the European Union, the U.K., and Canada over the course of 2025, after the U.S. approved it in 2024. Plenty more jurisdictions are evaluating the treatment today. In the U.S., label extensions (the expansion of FDA approval for use) mean Vertex can treat 95% of people with cystic fibrosis in the country today.

Vertex has expanded into treating other conditions. Povetacicept, an investigational recombinant fusion protein therapeutic, continues to show best-in-class potential as a treatment for three chronic autoimmune diseases: IgA nephropathy (IgAN), primary membranous nephropathy (pMN), and generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG). The FDA has granted its Breakthrough Therapy Designation for IgAN, with potential approval by Nov. 30 this year, as well as Fast Track Designation for pMN. If approved across the IgAN, pMN, and gMG indications, peak annual sales could reach $4 billion to $7 billion, according to equity analyst firm Argus.

For fiscal 2026, Vertex sales should rise about 10% to $13.3 billion, with net income coming in roughly even with 2025.

Both of these pharma giants can make a compelling argument for investors' dollars. Johnson & Johnson offers better value, based on its P/E and P/S ratios, and stands as the better buy for 2027.

#2916. Disney: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Capital discipline now decides which new theme park projects get built, a test that will shape Disney's returns long after 2026.

On its fiscal fourth-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 17, 2026, Disney (DIS -1.48%) announced 16% growth in full-year adjusted earnings per share, a financial metric that reflects profitability by excluding one-time charges. Expanding margins across theme parks and streaming services position the business to generate steady long-term returns. Three things management said stand out.

Management is overhauling operational workflows to reduce administrative overhead and improve corporate speed. Cutting structural expenses allows the company to fund core expansion on direct-to-consumer or streaming digital platforms without overburdening its balance sheet.

The company is executing a major multiyear capacity expansion across its domestic and international resort properties. Management evaluates every new attraction against strict hurdle rates to ensure new park investments yield sustainable cash flows.

Domestic resort bookings show steady demand heading into the first quarter, reflecting stable consumer engagement. Management monitors attendance and guest spending to gauge momentum across domestic properties.

#2911. Amer Sports: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Arc'teryx plans to grow North American stores from 77 toward 200-plus, a long-term bet that depends on managing inventory as spending scales.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 17, 2026, Amer Sports (AS +3.92%) raised its full-year 2026 revenue growth guidance to a range of 25% to 26%. Expanding gross margins give the business room to fund marketing and retail expansion across its growth engines. Three comments from management highlight what matters most for long-term investors.

Arc'teryx, the company's technical apparel brand, is expanding its retail footprint to capture direct-to-consumer demand (sales made directly through owned stores and websites). Management sees long-term potential to expand its North American store count from 77 locations to more than 200 doors.

Salomon, the company's outdoor footwear brand, is broadening distribution into specialized sneaker stores across North America. Expanding retail partnerships with modern sneaker sellers helps the brand reach younger urban consumers.

Amer Sports is directing gross profit gains into long-term marketing, store openings, and digital platforms. Reinvesting earnings supports sustainable growth across key brands rather than maximizing short-term operating margins.

Sales momentum is building across global outdoor markets, and executive optimism is evident in updated full-year forecasts.

The company maintains a strong competitive position through high brand loyalty and expanded direct-to-consumer distribution. Long-term performance relies on executing store expansions, preserving pricing discipline, and managing inventory flow as operational investments scale.

#2910. 3 Dividend Kings to Buy in November That Could Pay You for the Rest of Your Life

Walmart, Lowe's, and Genuine Parts have raised their dividends annually for more than 50 straight years, and each one is making big moves this month.

This is a big week for two retail chains that tens of millions of Americans walk into regularly without a second thought. Lowe's reports its third-quarter results on Wednesday before the opening of trading, and Walmart follows on Thursday.

Both are Dividend Kings — companies that have raised their dividends every year for at least 50 consecutive years. Not just paid one, but raised it, through recessions, oil shocks, and every ugly market in between. Fewer than 60 companies are currently on that list. Another is Genuine Parts, the company behind NAPA Auto Parts, which I also view as a smart buy this month.

That steadiness is what makes all three worth a look if you want income streams that will likely still be growing decades from now. When a dividend-hiking run gets that long, the annual increases become more than habit — they become a central part of the investment thesis, and no board wants to break the streak. Here's what each of these companies is doing right now to keep theirs going.

Walmart (WMT -0.69%) raised its dividend for the 53rd straight year in February. CFO John David Rainey called the raise "a proof point of our continued confidence in our business performance and forward momentum." What I like more is how it's treating shoppers this fall.

Walmart paid billions in tariffs during the earlier phases of President Donald Trump’s trade wars, and it raised prices to pass them along to customers. But the Supreme Court ruled early in 2026 that those tariffs were illegal, and as a result, Walmart alone got back about $2.9 billion in tariff refunds this year. Instead of pocketing the cash, it has been cutting prices. It had more than 11,000 rollbacks running in the second quarter, up from about 7,200 three months earlier.

"We're investing heavily in price because customers need us to, and because we believe it drives market share gains over time," CEO John Furner said.

That's also the catch for Thursday. Walmart told investors to expect only 2% to 4% operating income growth this quarter because of all those price reductions, and Furner admitted customers are "still feeling some pressure."

The retailer’s holiday push is aimed squarely at stretched budgets. Walmart's holiday meal basket feeds 10 people for $35, with more food than last year's. It's running three sales events between Nov. 12 and Dec. 2, and now bundles Walmart+ and Sam's Club Plus memberships for $120 a year.

Walmart is getting better at helping people shop, too. The number of customers using Sparky, its AI shopping assistant, is up 70% from last year, and they spend 40% more per order than shoppers who skip it. Furner described one shopper asking Sparky for a week of high-protein meals and receiving recipes, with every ingredient added to the cart with one click.

Walmart sells groceries and household basics, the heart of consumer staples, so it doesn't need a strong economy to keep its customers. I'm buying the stock before Thursday.

Lowe's (LOW -1.99%) has paid a dividend every quarter since going public in 1961, and it has raised that payout every year for more than half a century.

This year has been harder than many. Home improvement is a consumer discretionary business, and people may put off building a new patio or renovating a kitchen when gas prices are unusually high.

"Elevated fuel prices combined with broader economic uncertainty have influenced household budgets," CEO Marvin Ellison said in August. At the time, Lowe's said it expected sales at existing stores to be roughly flat this year.

Contractors, online orders, and installation services kept it growing anyway, for a fifth straight quarter. Ellison said that growth came "despite pressure in discretionary DIY spending." The shelves are changing to match. Lowe's is adding more than 150 new DeWalt items and bringing Traeger grills into stores and online nationwide. It also bought two businesses that serve builders and contractors, though high mortgage rates are slowing new home construction, which is hurting the performance of both of those acquired businesses for now.

The retailer is also finding new ways to reach people. Its AI shopping assistant, Mylow, has fielded more than 25 million questions from customers and employees, and online shoppers who use it buy at triple the rate of those who don't.

On Nov. 10, Lowe's became the exclusive home improvement partner of Bilt, a rewards platform with more than 8 million members who earn points on their housing payments.

"Home improvement is personal," said Lowe's Chief Marketing Officer Jen Wilson, and the deal lets Lowe's "meet customers in a community they already know and love." A curated Lowe's marketplace inside Bilt is planned for early 2027.

The DIY slowdown is already built into its forecast, so Wednesday’s results don't need to be great, just steady. I'd buy ahead of it and Lowe's Dec. 9 investor conference.

Genuine Parts (GPC -1.29%) has raised its dividend for 70 straight years. Early next year, it splits into two companies: the NAPA-led auto parts business, which keeps the Genuine Parts name, and Motion, its industrial parts supplier.

Court Carruthers, who will run the auto side, said the split gives it "greater flexibility and focus to successfully compete across our global markets." He also put the business plainly: "The mechanic in the garage turning a wrench. This is the core of who GPC serves and how the business was built." He praised NAPA's independent store owners, too: "They know their markets. They know their customers. They build trusted local relationships."

Behind the counter, NAPA just opened its first heavily automated distribution center in eastern Pennsylvania to get parts to those garages faster.

The weak spot for the company is drivers' wallets.

"Our experience indicates that higher energy prices from the Iran conflict are weakening consumer sentiment," CFO Bert Nappier said. Comparable sales to its retail customers fell 3% last quarter, and the company trimmed its profit forecast for the year.

CEO Will Stengel, who will lead Motion, said the company remains "on track to launch GPC and Motion as two independent, leading companies in the first quarter of 2027." The next quarterly dividend goes out to shareholders on Jan. 5, with an ex-dividend date of Dec. 4.

#2908. Baidu: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Baidu is trading near-term search ad revenue for a stickier platform, a bet that will determine whether its cloud momentum lasts.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, Baidu (BIDU -3.77%) announced that revenue from its artificial intelligence (AI) cloud infrastructure unit grew 94% year over year. The expansion of enterprise cloud workloads is shifting the business model toward recurring, long-term contracts. This structural pivot offers clear revenue visibility while search ad monetization undergoes a deliberate transformation. Three key takeaways from management's comments highlight where the business is headed.

Enterprise customers aren't just experimenting with AI anymore. They're embedding model workflows into daily operational software, which expands contract terms and increases upfront prepayments.

Custom semiconductor design lowers dependence on third-party suppliers. Proprietary hardware reduces computing unit costs while enabling end-to-end software and infrastructure optimization.

Management is overhauling traditional search by adding interactive, conversational features to deepen user engagement. Near-term ad revenue is taking a back seat to build a stickier product platform.

Cloud computing momentum is accelerating across enterprise workflows, and leadership isn't hiding its satisfaction with the trajectory. In-house chip development and software agent tools provide structural tailwinds for future value. Management is pacing capital expenditures to match customer demand and speed up cash recovery.

Unlocking long-term value requires balancing technical investments with disciplined capital allocation. Management is directing capital toward high-return opportunities while returning cash to shareholders through share repurchases and planned dividends.

#2903. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

#2900. Oaktree (OCSL) Q4 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Nonaccrual exposure fell 44% while the company returned to the bond market.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Oaktree Specialty Lending (OCSL -2.16%) management emphasized debt reduction and balance sheet flexibility as key achievements for the fiscal year. The company reduced its nonaccrual exposure by 44% in dollar terms through successful credit workouts and proceeds from repayments. Management successfully accessed the debt markets with a $300 million unsecured bond offering, extending its maturity profile and maintaining leverage at the midpoint of its target range. The portfolio remains centered on senior secured lending, with first-lien debt making up nearly 80% of total investments and a focus on larger, resilient companies with median EBITDA of $180 million.

Operator: Welcome, and thank you for joining Oaktree Specialty Lending Corporation's Fourth Fiscal Quarter 2026 Conference Call. Today's conference is being recorded. [Operator Instructions] I will now turn the call over to Alison Mermey, OCSL's Head of Investor Relations. Please go ahead.

Alison Friedman Mermey: Thank you, operator. Our fourth fiscal quarter and full year 2026 earnings release, which we issued this morning, along with the accompanying slide presentation, can be accessed on the Investors section of our website, oaktreespecialtylending.com. Before we begin, I want to remind you that the comments on today's call include forward-looking statements reflecting current views with respect to, among other things, future operating results and financial performance. Actual results could differ materially from those implied or expressed in the forward-looking statements. Please refer to the relevant SEC filings for a discussion of these factors in further detail. Oaktree undertakes no duty to update or revise any forward-looking statements.

I'd also like to advise you that nothing on this call constitutes an offer to sell or solicitation of an offer to purchase any interest in an Oaktree fund. Investors and others should note that OCSL uses the Investors section of its corporate website to announce material information. The company encourages investors, the media and others to review information that it shares on its website. On today's call, Matt Pendo, President of OCSL, will begin with a progress report on fiscal 2026 priorities and an overview of our fourth fiscal quarter results. Armen Panossian, CEO and Co-Chief Investment Officer, will then provide a market update.

Raghav Khanna, Co-Chief Investment Officer, will cover portfolio activity; and Chris McKown, CFO and Treasurer, will close with a review of our financial results before we open the call for questions. Now I'll turn the call over to Matt Pendo, President of OCSL. Matt?

Mathew Pendo: Thank you, Alison, and good morning, everyone. I would like to begin by reflecting on the progress we've made against our 2 main priorities from this past year: number one, reducing non-accruals; and number two, keeping our balance sheet flexible. I'll then cover our financial highlights and our dividend. Starting with non-accruals. At the end of our fiscal year, non-accruals were approximately 1.8% of the total debt portfolio at fair value. This was down about 120 basis points from a year ago and relatively unchanged from June. In dollar terms, non-accruals at fair value have fallen from $81 million a year ago to $45 million today, a reduction of 44%.

Importantly, nearly 85% of the decline in non-accrual dollars over the past year came from proceeds received and investments returning to accrual status. We believe this reflects our team's ability to successfully work through challenging credits. Raghav will walk through additional updates to non-accrual positions in his remarks. There were several positive developments to our balance sheet this quarter, including an update to our ratings outlook and a bond offering. First, in August, Moody's revised our outlook to stable and kept our Baa3 rating unchanged. Second, in September, we priced $300 million of 5-year unsecured notes due 2031 with a 7% coupon and a spread of 240 basis points over U.S. treasuries.

After swapping the floating rate, the equivalent spread to SOFR is 258 basis points. This was our first bond issuance in over a year, and we were pleased with the outcome in a choppy market backdrop. Demand was strong with a final order book more than 3x the size of the deal and included new OCSL bond investors. We want to thank the fixed income community for their support on this transaction. The incremental cost of the new financing is less than $0.05 per share per quarter. After the January 2027 notes are repaid at maturity using borrowings under our revolving credit facility, we will be left with a well-staggered maturity profile, and our next debt maturity is February 2029.

We maintained conservative leverage during the quarter, ending September 30 at 1.00 net debt to equity. Over the course of fiscal year 2026, we maintain net leverage between 0.97x and 1.07x, well inside the long-term target range of 0.9x to 1.25x. Liquidity was approximately $1.04 billion at quarter end. That figure is temporarily elevated because the January notes have not yet been repaid. Pro forma for that repayment, liquidity is approximately $689 million, in line with last quarter and a year ago. Turning to financial highlights for our fourth fiscal quarter and fiscal year. Adjusted net investment income was $29.6 million or approximately $0.34 per share, down from $32.2 million or $0.37 per share in the prior quarter.

This decrease primarily reflected a lower average portfolio balance and a higher income-based incentive fee. For the full fiscal year, adjusted net investment income was $131.7 million or $1.49 per share compared to $151.3 million or $1.76 per share for the 2025 fiscal year. Now turning to the dividend. Our Board declared a total cash dividend of $0.32 per share for the quarter, composed of a base cash dividend of $0.30 per share and a supplemental dividend of $0.02 per share. The dividends are payable in cash on December 31, 2026, to stockholders of record on December 15, 2026.

As a reminder, we adjusted the base dividend in May to be covered by earnings at our current conservative leverage and maintain the supplemental dividend equal to 50% of adjusted net investment income in excess of the base dividend. Dividends declared in fiscal 2026 totaled $1.39 per share, which represented 108% of adjusted net investment income. As we look to fiscal 2027, our top priorities are unchanged: resolve non-accruals, exit non-income-producing equity positions and redeploy those proceeds into performing assets. With net leverage of 1.00x and nearly $700 million of pro forma liquidity, we have the flexibility to be opportunistic as new investments and strategic opportunities arise.

With that, I'll turn the call over to Armen to discuss the market environment.

Armen Panossian: Thank you, Matt. Since this is a fiscal year-end call, I want to compare where we stood a year ago with where we are today. Last year, the Fed had just cut rates in September and October, and the market expected additional rate cuts in 2026, along with an upswing in M&A activity. Neither of those predictions materialized. Instead, markets saw increased volatility in early 2026 amid concerns of AI disruption, geopolitical uncertainty and renewed inflation pressures. It's a good reminder of why Oaktree's investment philosophy is tied to fundamental credit analysis rather than macro forecasting.

Fast forward to today, with Brent crude rising above $100 per barrel during the September quarter and large AI-related CapEx adding to inflation pressures, the Fed instead raised rates by 25 basis points. That put the federal funds rate at 3.75% to 4% and the 10-year treasury yield moved above the symbolic 5% threshold, reaching its highest level in nearly 2 decades. A year ago, the debate was how far rates would fall. Today, it is how long they stay high and how high.

Our view is that a move in short-term base rates from approximately 4% to 5% would remain manageable and could be beneficial to investor returns as most of the increase would flow through to net investment income. For OCSL, 91% of our debt investments are floating rate and a higher base rate increases contractual interest income as loans reset. The other important factor is whether our borrowers can withstand higher rates. We assess each borrower's interest coverage across a range of base rate scenarios and build cushion into the capital structure at underwriting. Based on our borrowers' last 12 months of results, weighted average interest coverage is 2.3x.

If base rates rose by 100 basis points, coverage would still be about 2.1x, above the roughly 1.9x trough our portfolio experienced in late 2023 and early 2024. Against the change in the rate backdrop, credit market outcomes in the quarter were uneven. U.S. leveraged loans returned nearly 2%, supported by floating rate coupons that limit sensitivity to rising rates, while high-yield bonds declined by almost 2% as treasury yields rose. Corporate fundamentals remain broadly resilient, but the weakest leverage credit lagged, reflecting increasingly selective investor appetite and the growing strain of elevated borrowing costs. On deal activity, direct lending volume rebounded slightly in September, although year-to-date deal flow and average deal size remained below that of last year.

Capital is still competitive for most transactions despite a pullback from many of the largest nontraded BDCs, meaning supply and demand are relatively balanced. Spreads on new sponsor-backed first lien loans held at SOFR plus 500 to 550 basis points for several months, about 50 to 100 basis points wider than that at the end of 2025. As the broadly syndicated loan market continued to tighten in the quarter, the premium that direct lending new originations offered over new broadly syndicated loans widened to over 150 basis points. Putting it together, we remain focused on what we can control, and we feel well positioned even in a higher for longer and more volatile environment.

With that, I will turn the call over to Raghav for a review of our investment activity and portfolio.

Raghav Khanna: Thank you, Armen. I'll start with new originations and exits, then discuss non-accruals before closing with portfolio metrics. Turning to originations. New investment commitments totaled $179 million in the quarter compared with $206 million last quarter and $208 million in the fourth fiscal quarter of 2025. We have seen our pipeline build steadily since late September and originated about $80 million of new investment commitments in October. Weighted average yield on new debt investments during the quarter was 9.6%, with a spread on new originations of about 570 basis points, wider than the market spread range of 500 to 550.

One notable deal during the quarter was a first lien loan to Outcomes First Group, which appears as OFS Bidco on our schedule of investments. Outcomes First Group is the U.K.'s largest independent provider of schools for children and youth with special educational needs and disabilities. The total facility consisted of GBP 600 million of first lien debt with about 75% funded upfront, priced at SONIA plus 525 basis points with 1.5 points of OID. The transaction refinanced the company's existing debt and supports further expansion, including internationally. Oaktree's long-standing relationship with the sponsor, TPG, positioned Oaktree as the anchor lender.

The investment was underwritten and structured by our European private debt team that has deep local market knowledge and direct engagement with the management team. We like the company due to its long operating history, hard-to-replicate footprint, high quality of schools and strong revenue visibility. It also operates in an industry with several demand tailwinds that includes statutory funding obligations, a persistent structural supply shortage and high barriers to entry. This deal demonstrates the value of combining on-the-ground presence and regional expertise with global relationships. During the quarter, we also participated in 3 performing equity investments that increased our equity exposure to 5.5% of fair value from 3.9% last quarter.

These income-producing equity deals are highly structured with negotiated downside protection and offer compelling risk-adjusted returns for our portfolio. Moving now to exits and prepayments. Proceeds from prepayments, exits, other paydowns and sales were $198 million compared with $263 million last quarter and $177 million last year. We were repaid in full on several private investments during the quarter. Some borrowers were taken out in the broadly syndicated loan market and some were refinanced by other private lenders. A notable exit in the quarter was the repayment of our first lien term loan in RumbleOn, which appeared in our schedule of investments as RideNow Group, a publicly traded company that operates powersports retail dealerships and an e-commerce business.

We were the agent on the term loan, which originally funded in 2021 to finance RumbleOn's acquisition of the RideNow powersports platform. Integration challenges and weaker post-COVID consumer spending pressured EBITDA and put covenant compliance at risk, leading us to mark the loan down to 94% in the year 2023. Importantly, our highly structured loan prevented value leakage during the company's period of underperformance, and we worked collaboratively with the company on a turnaround plan involving new management, cost reductions, asset sales and capital raises.

These actions reposition the company for growth, and we were willing to provide relief to the company in the form of covenant resets and a maturity extension in exchange for debt paydowns during this time to rightsize the balance sheet. In September, RumbleOn refinanced its term loan and our remaining $35 million position was repaid at 101, including an exit fee, a year ahead of the 2027 maturity. This is another example of how our focused structuring capabilities, patience, collaboration with management teams and discipline yielded a positive outcome for OCSL stakeholders. Turning to non-accruals. At September 30, 6 companies were on nonaccrual, the same as last quarter and down from 10 a year ago.

Non-accruals represented approximately 1.8% of the total debt portfolio at fair value. This also represents 4% of the total debt portfolio at cost, which is down 20 basis points sequentially and 250 basis points from a year ago on a cost basis. One investment came off non-accrual, one new tranche in an existing non-accrual name was added and one new borrower was added. BayMark came off non-accrual following the resolution by lenders to recapitalize and stabilize the business. We added the first-out term loan of Pluralsight to non-accrual as the company continues to underperform expectations. As a reminder, in December 2025, we placed the second our term loan on non-accrual and marked our equity to 0.

The new name added to non-accrual during this quarter is BioXcel Therapeutics, a publicly traded biopharmaceutical company, but we expect a relatively quick resolution in the December quarter. BioXcel entered Chapter 11 in late August 2026 with an up to $125 million stocking horse proposal from Teva, a large global pharmaceutical company. It was a fast-moving court supervised process and in late October, the sale to Teva closed at a value in line with our carrying mark of [ 83 ]. Teva funded $57.5 million upfront at closing with the balance payable upon certain regulatory and commercial thresholds.

We're pleased to say that on November 13, Teva received FDA approval for BioXcel's drug, which has triggered the full $67.5 million additional milestone to the lenders. This is expected to be paid within 30 days and will lead to a realized value well in excess of our carrying mark. Closing out the discussion on non-accruals is an update on Thrasio. As a reminder, last quarter, 80% of the debt was paid down and the small amount that remained was returned to accrual status. In early November, the balance was fully paid down at par. Looking at total portfolio metrics as of September 30, 79.1% of the portfolio at fair value was first lien senior secured debt.

Weighted average yield on debt investments was 9.4% compared with 9.3% last quarter. The portfolio remains well diversified with 170 portfolio companies. The average debt position is approximately 62 basis points of the total portfolio at fair value and no single position exceeds 2.1%. The median EBITDA of our portfolio companies was approximately $180 million. Portfolio company weighted average leverage was 5.1x and the interest coverage was 2.3x, both metrics steady sequentially and compared to a year ago. On software, based on GICS industry group classification, software represents 20% of the portfolio at fair value, steady sequentially and down from 24% a year ago.

94% of our software positions are first lien and ARR-based loans represent just 0.8% of the portfolio. Our high AI risk software exposure remains relatively unchanged at approximately 3% of the performing debt portfolio. And with that, I'll turn the call over to Chris to review our financial results.

Christopher McKown: Thank you, Raghav. In our fourth fiscal quarter ended September 30, 2026, adjusted total investment income was $68.0 million, down from $69.2 million in the prior quarter. The sequential decrease primarily reflected a lower average portfolio balance. Fiscal year adjusted total investment income was $281.4 million compared with $315.4 million in the prior year. The year-over-year decrease was primarily attributable to lower reference rates and a lower average portfolio balance. The year-over-year decrease was primarily attributable to lower reference rates and a lower average portfolio balance. We delivered adjusted net investment income of $29.6 million or $0.34 per share compared with $32.2 million or $0.37 per share in the prior quarter.

The sequential decline was driven by lower total investment income and a higher income-based or Part 1 incentive fee, partially offset by lower interest and other expenses. For the full year, adjusted net investment income was $131.7 million or $1.49 per share compared with $151 million or $1.76 per share in fiscal 2025 with similar performance drivers as the quarterly results. On the incentive fee, we paid $4.8 million this quarter or approximately $0.05 per share compared with $2.4 million last quarter or $0.03 per share. A full quarterly incentive fee would have been approximately $6.0 million. So our total return hurdle reduced the fee by approximately $1.2 million or about $0.01 per share in the September quarter.

For the full year, incentive fee paid was $8.4 million or $0.10 per share. Over the last year, our total return hurdle reduced incentive fees by $16.1 million. We believe the mechanism is working as designed, aligning the fees Oaktree earns with the total returns shareholders receive. Beginning in the December quarter, the September rate hike will start to flow through to net investment income. With 91% of our debt investments floating rate and 100% of our debt floating or swapped to floating, the net impact is positive, all else equal. Although resets are somewhat staggered across the portfolio, we'll see the majority of the impact in our December quarter earnings with the full effect building from there.

Once fully reset, every 25 basis point increase in base rates adds a little over $0.05 per share per quarter to adjusted net investment income with a little over $0.02 per share annualized. Turning to NAV. NAV per share was $15.63 as of September 30, down 0.4% from $15.70 in June. PIK income represented approximately 7.3% of adjusted total investment income during the quarter, down from 7.8% last quarter. Approximately 75% of total PIK income relates to investments that were structured with the option to PIK at origination. Investments generating noncash income during the quarter are marked at a weighted average fair value of approximately 98%.

Our net leverage at quarter end was 1.00x compared with 1.02x last quarter and 0.97x a year ago. Our long-term target leverage range of 0.9x to 1.25x remains unchanged. Total debt outstanding at quarter end was $1.42 billion. As of September 30, the weighted average interest rate on debt outstanding was 6.2% compared with 5.9% last quarter and 6.5% a year ago. Adjusted for the new 2031 notes, paydown of the 2027 notes and a higher base rate, pro forma interest rate on debt is 6.4%. Unsecured debt represented approximately 88% of total debt at quarter end compared with 65% last quarter and 64% a year ago.

Pro forma for the expected repayment of the 2027 notes, unsecured debt would have been approximately 64% of total debt at quarter end. Our liquidity was approximately $1.04 billion, including $45 million of cash and $994 million of undrawn capacity under our credit facilities. Pro forma for the repayment of the 2027 notes at maturity, liquidity would have been approximately $689 million compared with $699 million last quarter and $695 million a year ago. Unfunded commitments, excluding those related to the joint ventures, were approximately $226 million. Ending with our joint ventures. Together, the JVs held approximately $547 million of investments across 144 portfolio companies and generated aggregate returns on equity of approximately 11.5% during the quarter.

Leverage at the JVs was 2.0x compared to 2.1x last quarter. With that, I'll turn the call back to the operator for Q&A.

Operator: [Operator Instructions] We have no questions at this time. I will now turn the call back to Alison for closing remarks. Opening the line again, I would like to re-prompt for questions. [Operator Instructions] Your first question comes from the line of Sean-Paul Adams with B. Riley Securities.

Sean-Paul Adams: It looks like PIK kind of trended down a little bit since the June quarter. Was that due to the restructuring? Or can you guys just walk me through the portfolio changes?

Christopher McKown: Sean-Paul, it's Chris. Thanks for the question. Yes, in terms of the downtick quarter-on-quarter, as mentioned in the prepared remarks, we did move BioXcel to non-accruals. That was part of the impact. We do expect a quick resolution there. And then the other factor, we had a royalty interest that had been picking previously that we exited during the quarter. Those are the 2 big drivers.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Ethan Kaye with Lucid Capital Markets.

Ethan Kaye: Just a quick one for me. Can you just kind of review capital allocation priorities? You talked about -- you have some room to kind of bring leverage higher if you want. You talked about remaining kind of flexible on the balance sheet, but just walk through balancing kind of deployment and repurchases and leverage, et cetera.

Mathew Pendo: Ethan, it's Matt. Thanks for the question. So we talk to the Board every quarter about capital allocation. I want to be thoughtful about it. Since we took over management, we have not repurchased shares for a couple of reasons. I think the primary is just desire to have capital to continue to invest in portfolios and attractive loans that we see. So that continues to be kind of priority #1. Over 2026, we ran leverage 0.97 to 1.07. As I mentioned in the call, our target is 0.9 to 1.25. We ended the year at 1x. So we definitely -- and as Chris kind of walked through our liquidity and capital, we have plenty of capital.

So I think our primary focus is to deploy and to attract investment opportunities. We have the capital. We're comfortable taking our leverage up within the range we've done in the last year, 0.97, 1.07. So I think that's where we're going to be focused.

#2896. Chevron Says AI Has Already Captured Over $100 Million in Value. Here's Where It Might Find an Even Bigger Payoff.

Chevron isn’t a tech company, but it’s already accruing benefits from use of artificial intelligence (AI).

To say that artificial intelligence (AI) stocks are prominent and top of mind for many investors is an understatement. Still, hefty market capitalizations and popularity don't always paint the full picture.

Sure, plenty of companies and high-ranking executives at those corporations are using AI, but many currently struggle to generate tangible profit benefits from the technology. The harsh reality is the vast majority of corporate AI adopters are spending on the technology with little to show for it. Chevron (CVX -0.47%) isn't part of that group.

Chevron is a prime example of a major company benefiting from AI adoption. Image source: Getty Images.

When it reported third-quarter earnings last month, Chevron told analysts and investors it had generated $100 million in year-to-date "operational efficiency gains" backed by AI initiatives. Chevron's AI benefits include a 50% reduction in loss production events in the Denver-Julesburg Basin. Those data points may be signs that Chevron is onto something with its AI ambitions.

No one will mistake Chevron for a technology company, and that's OK. Its primary business is the exploration and production of oil and natural gas, but that makes Chevron (and some of its peers) ideal for broader AI deployment.

Getting down to the heart of the matter, the lifeblood of the energy industry is finding new reserves and efficiently extracting more oil and gas from already tapped basins, offshore deposits, and oilfields. Oil and natural gas prospecting is data- and labor-intensive, time-consuming, and not cheap. AI can improve those scenarios so much that Chevron has its own AI platform, ApEX.

Two interesting facts about ApEX for Chevron investors to consider: First, this is very much an "in-house" product, meaning the oil major is saving some money by not farming out its most important AI needs to a third-party vendor. Second, ApEX was initially tested in deep waters in the Gulf of Mexico. That shows confidence in the platform, as deep-water offshore drilling is often cost-intensive and potentially risky.

Those are points to keep in mind as Chevron looks to solidify long-term earnings growth by probing new fields in locations such as Venezuela while potentially lifting output at domestic oilfields such as the Bakken Shale and the Permian Basin. If there's one thing oil companies love, it's the ability to pull more oil and gas than previously expected from the locations in which they're already operational.

So, in answering "where" in terms of the locations where Chevron can drive AI benefits, the answer could well be "everywhere."

Some industries are struggling with AI adoption, but as Chevron proves, that's not the case in the oil and gas space. Some experts believe one reason for successful AI deployment in the energy sector is that it's a real-world, not a virtual, application.

Beyond that, there's a belief that dedicated energy-sector AI adopters will leverage the technology to improve efficiencies, cut costs, and deliver tangible earnings before interest and taxes (EBIT) benefits. And for those worried about the environmental impact of the oil and gas industry, companies such as Chevron can dramatically reduce their carbon footprints by using AI.

The good news is that Chevron is already reaping benefits from AI use, and there's likely more to come in the long term.

#2895. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

#2894. Good News: "Trumpflation" Might Go Away Soon. Here's What This Means for Stocks.

Most of America’s recent inflation came from two presidential policies. But the future of inflation might be brighter in 2027.

A big reason why Donald Trump was reelected as U.S. president in 2024 is that voters wanted him to fix inflation. High costs of living have dogged the U.S. economy since the pandemic, with the inflation rate reaching highs over 9% in 2022.

The Fed has set 2% inflation as its official target. But for the past several years, inflation has been higher than that. The Fed's preferred metric for inflation, the Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE) price index, has been above target since February 2021 -- almost six years.

Inflation has been a challenge to countries around the world and to U.S. presidents of both parties ever since 2020. The pandemic caused global supply chain disruptions and massive amounts of government borrowing to get through the crisis.

Unfortunately, it seems that Trump's policies in his second term as president have largely made inflation worse. In the recent November midterm election, exit polls indicated that a majority of voters blamed Trump policies for higher prices.

Let's look at why "Trumpflation" might be a big culprit in American consumers' current dissatisfaction about the economy -- and why new Vanguard research shows signs of hope for lower inflation and strong economic growth in 2027.

Trump imposed sweeping new tariffs in 2025 that effectively act as a tax on imports, driving up costs for businesses and consumers. Trump started a war with Iran in February 2026, which has caused global disruptions to the oil market, raising oil prices and driving up costs for household energy bills, gasoline, and diesel fuel. Higher energy costs spill over into the entire economy, driving up prices for airfare, farm fertilizer, food on grocery store shelves, and any consumer goods shipped by truck.

Although these Trump policies have not been severe enough to cause a recession, they have caused a noticeable uptick in inflation. This chart shows what has happened to inflation since Donald Trump took office in January 2025. Look at that big spike after February 2026, when the Iran war started.

US Consumer Price Index YoY data by YCharts

It's possible that inflation might have already "gotten better" -- that America would already have lower inflation in line with the Fed's 2% target -- if not for Donald Trump. According to Motley Fool research, higher energy costs are the biggest factor behind why overall inflation remains so high. Energy inflation was nearly 10 times higher than overall inflation as of September.

But if you want lower inflation, the future is looking more hopeful.

Recent Vanguard research found that inflation is being driven by three major price shocks: tariffs, energy prices, and artificial intelligence (AI) industry demand. Two of those driving forces were caused by Trump's own decisions -- "Trumpflation."

However, Vanguard's analysis has a hopeful message. Tariff effects on inflation are likely to fade soon, as the one-time increase in tariff costs gets “rolled off” from the official year-over-year inflation data. (This doesn't mean the tariffs go away, but the way they are measured will change; the tariff effect on inflation is already built into the economy and will not drive inflation higher.) By April 2027, just from this tariff "roll-off" effect, Vanguard forecasts that core inflation could drop to 2.5%. As of September 2026, the Fed's core inflation rate (PCE price index excluding food and energy) was 3.1%.

What about energy prices? Vanguard's analysis suggests that high energy prices might not subside as quickly, but unless they rise again, their contribution to inflation could be more limited in 2027. Vanguard forecasts a core inflation rate of 2.5% by the end of 2027, with 3% gross domestic product (GDP) growth and only 4.4% unemployment.

There's no guarantee that Vanguard's analysis is correct. However, if you agree that the biggest inflation effects of Trump's tariffs are already "baked in," and that the energy price surge from the Iran war might slowly go away over the next few months to a year, it's reasonable to think that inflation could go lower in 2027.

If both "Trumpflation" policy shocks of tariffs and energy costs go away soon (and Vanguard believes they will), that means inflation might already be closer to the Fed's 2% target. That means fewer Fed rate hikes, lower interest rates, and possibly higher stock prices. The economy could keep growing strong into 2027 and beyond, without getting stuck in "stagflation."

If you're bullish on a future of lower inflation, buying an S&P 500 exchange-traded fund (ETF) is usually a good choice. The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -1.04%) holds the 500 largest publicly traded stocks in America. The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF has delivered annualized returns of about 12% in the past five years.

If America can get to the other side of the recent spikes in inflation and keep the economy growing strong, this S&P 500 ETF is likely to benefit from that future growth.

#2889. The Nasdaq-100 Is Down More Than 10% From Its High, but the Broad Market Is Down Far Less. Here's How Gaps Like This Have Ended Since 1986.

Twelve past gaps like this one go back to 1986. Did the broad market usually follow tech down?

The broad market, as measured by the S&P 500 (^GSPC -1.03%), has held up much better than tech stocks this fall. The index closed at 7,561.67 on Friday, Nov. 13, just 3% or so under its Oct. 6 record of 7,818.93.

The Nasdaq-100, which leans heavily on big tech and growth stocks, reached its own high close of 31,224.47 the same day. But by Nov. 13, it had fallen to 27,943.74, around 10.5% under the peak.

A gap like this doesn't come along very often. Digging through both indexes' daily closes, I counted 12 past cases since the mid-1980s, plus one in July this year.

But when tech stocks drop this far on their own, does the broad market usually follow them down?

I used Yahoo Finance's daily closes, which don't include dividends, and took each index's best close in the prior 252 trading sessions (around a year) as its high.

A day counted if the Nasdaq-100 closed 10% or more below that level and the S&P 500 closed at most 4% under its own. And so a long slide wouldn't count again and again, a new case needed to start at least six months after the last qualifying day of the one before.

The Nasdaq-100's daily history on Yahoo begins in October 1985, and the first case turned up around a year later, on Oct. 30, 1986.

Most of the others came before 2001, with two in 1997. Three more followed in 2004, 2005, and 2006. After that, the pattern didn't return until March 2021.

This year's case started on July 29. Because the current gap opened on Nov. 2, only about three months later, it doesn't count as a new case by my six-month rule.

Three months after each of the 12 past cases began, the S&P 500 was higher in 11 of them, and the median gain was around 7%. A year later, the index was up again in 11 of the 12, and the median gain came to about 11%. Across every trading day since late 1986, for comparison, it finished the next three months higher around 70% of the time, typically rising about 3%. And it ended the next year higher about 79% of the time, with gains of about 12% at the median.

In other words, the three months after those gaps went better than normal. But the following year looked a lot like an ordinary one.

Most of those gaps closed because the Nasdaq-100 recovered, not because the broader market caught up. In 11 of the 12 cases, it hit a new one-year high within 12 months, after a median wait of 81 days. The July case went the same way, with the Nasdaq-100 setting a new high 55 days later.

Of course, the broad market didn't always stay calm. In six of the 12 cases, the S&P 500 experienced a 10% or greater decline at one point in the following year, including the 1987 crash. Still, it finished the year up in five of the six.

The only case that went badly began on April 3, 2000, a week after the Nasdaq-100's March 27 record close.

Within two weeks, the S&P 500 was more than 10% below its peak. It was down about 2% three months later and roughly 27% a year later. The Nasdaq-100 fell about 66% in that year, and it didn't close above its March 2000 peak again until November 2015.

What set 2000 apart was the run before it. In the previous 252 sessions, the Nasdaq-100 had climbed about 84%, versus about 14% for the S&P 500. As of Nov. 13, both indexes were up about 10% over the last 252 sessions.

Sure, a strong run alone didn't decide things. The Nasdaq-100 had gained about 67% in the year before the February 1999 case, and the S&P 500 still rose about 9% over the next 12 months. But I think today's gap looks much more like the ordinary cases than 2000.

For buy-and-hold investors in an S&P 500 index fund like the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -1.04%), which closed near $693 on Nov. 13, the history arguably weighs against seeing the gap as a warning. Tech stocks might keep dropping for a while, and this gap may take months to close. But in most of these cases, the broad market didn't follow them down.

I'd consider adding to a fund like VOO now, maybe little by little. Still, 12 cases isn't many, and one was 2000.

#2886. Nvidia Has a $118 Billion Promise Tucked Inside Its Blowout Quarter. Here's What It Means for the Stock

Nvidia crushed expectations again. But its report also shows a growing pile of promises to help customers pay for the data centers that run its chips.

Nvidia (NVDA -0.44%) delivered another huge quarter on Tuesday. Revenue rose 103% from a year earlier to $115.8 billion, and the company forecast $130 billion for the current quarter, according to its earnings release. Shares rose about 5% after hours to around $163.

It was Nvidia's first report since co-founder Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week. But for long-term investors, one of the most important details sits in the fine print of its CFO commentary.

Nvidia disclosed up to $118 billion in guarantees, meaning it may have to pay third parties "if certain of our partners default on their obligations."

Almost all of that, $105 billion, supports SB Energy's PORTS-Pike Technology Campus in Ohio, which will "exclusively host Nvidia infrastructure under 20-year leases to OpenAI." The remaining $3.5 billion is for neocloud data centers, and $9.5 billion is for power and systems.

Neoclouds are smaller companies that rent out AI computing power. Nvidia also has $32 billion in separate commitments to them, which let it share in their rental revenue.

For scale, $118 billion is more than twice the $53.5 billion in cash and marketable debt securities Nvidia held at the end of the quarter.

Nvidia was direct about the reason. Neoclouds and AI labs "are seeing extraordinary demand for AI infrastructure, yet many are growing faster than their balance sheets and long-term credit profiles can support," it said in the Tuesday filing.

In other words, some customers want more Nvidia computing power than they can finance on their own. By backing the land, power, and buildings, Nvidia helps them raise money, and the chips that fill those buildings come from Nvidia.

The guarantees come with conditions. The Ohio guarantees "take effect phase by phase" as each building is finished, with the first expected in fiscal 2029. Exposure then shrinks over each 20-year lease as OpenAI pays rent. Nvidia said it has not made any payments on its guarantees.

On the earnings call, interim CEO Colette Kress said that if a partner is delayed or runs into trouble before a building is ready, "our guarantee doesn't exist anymore." She added that Nvidia is "rigorously evaluating future investments, commitments and guarantees" as other sources of capital become available.

The same pattern appears on the balance sheet. Accounts receivable, the money customers owe Nvidia, reached $81 billion, more than double the $38.5 billion at the end of January. Nvidia said that this was partly reflected in "extended payment terms on large, multi-quarter agreements with certain investment-grade customers."

None of this changes the core story. Nvidia expects revenue to grow about 75% next fiscal year, and Kress said demand is "far exceeding supply."

But it does change the risk. If AI spending cools, the damage may not stop at slower chip sales. Nvidia could also be on the hook for some of its partners' obligations, and it could take longer to collect what it's owed.

For investors in AI stocks, the guarantee total is worth watching every quarter. If it keeps climbing faster than sales, that could be a sign Nvidia is increasingly paying to create its own demand.
