#2883. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2876. Spurs' 12-game winning streak: What to know about Victor Wembanyama and the NBA's hottest team

The NBA's hottest team resides in South Texas. After knocking off the Utah Jazz in Salt Lake City on Sunday night, the San Antonio Spurs enter a new week riding a 12-game winning streak — the franchise's longest in more than a decade. Last season featured only two streaks longer than the Spurs' dominant dozen: a 16-game early-season jaunt by the Oklahoma City Thunder (which, as you might remember, these Spurs snapped in the NBA Cup semifinals) and a 13-game run by the Detroit Pistons … who, as luck would have it, will visit the Spurs in San Antonio on Tuesday night.

When a team goes on a winning streak this long, there's plenty of credit to go around. Let's highlight 12 things worth knowing about the Spurs' stampede to the top of the Western Conference — one for each win in the streak! — starting with … I mean, come on, where else can you start?

After the New York Knicks ended his phenomenal third season just short of a championship, Wembanyama entered the summer with a bitter taste in his mouth … and he seems to have entered the new campaign intent on washing it out by devouring everything in his path.

Wembanyama enters Tuesday's action third in the NBA in scoring (a career-high 28.6 points per game) and fifth in rebounding (11.6 caroms a night) while, for the fourth straight season, leading the league in blocked shots (4.3 swats per contest). He hasn't forsaken the 3-point arc, but he's taking a higher share of his shots than ever directly at the rim, and he's converting them at a predictably elite rate, shooting 79.5% in the restricted area — tops among players to take at least 50 point-blank shots.

(Third on that list, by the way? Friggin' Dylan Harper. Don't worry: We'll get back to him.)

Big Vic is averaging — averaging! — 4.64 dunks per game. That's nearly two more flushes per game than last season (2.78), which was already a career high. As tactics go, "Let's get the biggest guy as close to the basket as possible and let him just put the ball into it" seems like a pretty smart strategy, and the Spurs have been reaping the benefits of it, scoring a scorching 126.6 points per 100 non-garbage-time possessions in Wemby's minutes, according to Cleaning the Glass — a mark that would rank head-and-shoulders above the Los Angeles Lakers' league-leading rate of offensive efficiency thus far.

When it comes to "season-opening heaters," most of the attention has gone to the man most responsible for that No. 1-ranked Lakers offense, Luka Dončić — and understandably so. It's worth noting, though, that the big fella is currently on pace to shatter the all-time record for player efficiency rating while leading the NBA in win shares, value over replacement player and total plus-minus; the Spurs have outscored opponents by 257 points in Wemby's 417 minutes, equivalent to a staggering 29.5 points-per-100.

Watch Wembanyama on a nightly basis — not just the "did he just finger-roll from past the free-throw line?!?" highlights, but the possession after possession where he completely warps the opposition's defense and defangs its offense just by standing there — and you come away feeling like the 22-year-old's control of the game is, or at least can be, total.

"Opponents can't really put me in a situation where I can't answer," Wembanyama recently told reporters. "So now all I have to do is not beat myself."

For more than three weeks, he hasn't — and, in a related story, nobody's beaten the Spurs, either.

All right, take it easy there, Thanos. (Not sure if you've heard, but it's all about Dr. Doom right now.)

Generally speaking, if you're in the top 10 on both ends of the floor, you can consider yourself a contender; if you're in the top five on both ends, you're in the inner circle. San Antonio enters Tuesday's action ranked second in the NBA in points scored and allowed per possession, according to Cleaning the Glass.

Where'd the Spurs finish last year, you might ask? Oh, I'm so glad you did. They were … third in points scored per possession, and third in points allowed per possession. If that was good enough to get them to the Finals, maybe bumping up to second both ways will be enough to get them to the top of the mountain this time?

San Antonio has a point differential of +180 for the season — the 21st-highest total ever through the first 14 games of a campaign, according to Stathead. Of the 20 teams above these Spurs, 17 made at least the conference finals; eight of them won it all.

Here's another: The Spurs are blitzing their opponents by 15.2 points per 100 non-garbage-time possessions thus far — the best net rating in the sport. Since the 2003-04 season, only four teams have finished a season outscoring the opposition by more than 12 points-per-100. All four of them — the 2007-08 Boston Celtics, 2014-15 and 2016-17 Golden State Warriors, and the 2024-25 Thunder — went on to win the NBA championship.

According to Jared Dubin's adjusted efficiency metrics at Last Night in Basketball, which measure how much better or worse a team's offense and defense were than the league's average unit in that season, the 2026-27 Spurs currently have a net rating 11.4 points-per-100 better than league average. That ranks third in Dubin's database, which goes all the way back to the ABA-NBA merger in 1976 — behind only Michael Jordan's 1995-96 Chicago Bulls (who won the title) … and this year's Boston Celtics, who (Monday's loss to the Orlando Magic notwithstanding) have been pretty awesome this year.

San Antonio's average margin of victory thus far is 12.86 points per game. Only one team has ever finished a full season higher: the '24-25 Thunder (+12.87) … who, again, won the title.

According to Basketball-Reference's Simple Rating System — a metric "that takes into account average point differential and strength of schedule" — this year's Spurs are 12.75 points better than league average. That's on pace to be the highest SRS rating in Basketball-Reference's database, ahead of teams like the '24-25 Thunder, 1970-71 Milwaukee Bucks, '95-96 Bulls and 1971-72 Los Angeles Lakers — all of whom (say it with me) won the title.

It's early, obviously, and 14 games is too small a sample to draw any definitive conclusions. This much, though, seems clear. If folks start digging into the historical record in search of points of comparison to what you're doing, and they keep coming up with teams that won the NBA championship? You're probably doing something right.

The backcourt logjam that some pundits anticipated last season never really materialized in San Antonio. De'Aaron Fox and Stephon Castle started; rookie Harper mostly came off the bench; the trio shared the floor for just 25 minutes during the regular season and 44 minutes in the playoffs, and the Spurs made it all the way to the NBA Finals. Not actually a problem, right?

Well, that seemed less likely to be the case this season, after Harper authored a breakout performance in his first postseason — and followed it up by making it pretty clear that he wasn't particularly interested in continuing to come off the bench. That led Spurs head coach Mitch Johnson to decide to open this season with all three of his high-octane guards in the starting five, backstopped by Wembanyama with Devin Vassell operating as an ostensible small-ball power forward. The early returns have been explosive:

The Spurs have hammered opponents by 110 points in 141 minutes with Fox, Castle and Harper sharing the floor this season, according to PBP Stats. They're scoring and defending at league-best rates in those minutes … and yes, a lot of that stems from the fact that Wembanyama has been out there for every single one of them. It's also working, though, because the guards are each finding ways to get in where they fit in.

They're flying up the court, with more than 23% of San Antonio's offensive possessions coming in transition in the three-guard lineup, and those units scoring at near-league-best levels on the break. Castle and Harper are almost exclusively taking shots at the rim or from beyond the arc in those minutes, getting to either catch-and-shoot or attack downhill after an initial action bends the defense.

Fox and Castle, both questionable 3-point shooters, have both been knocking down well over 40% of their triples in these alignments. When the pick-your-poison wheel spins to a center guarding Castle, the Spurs have shown their work in developing counters to that scheme.

And while conventional wisdom would warn you against the defensive drawbacks of playing small, with no player listed taller than 6-foot-6 at the 1 through 4 spots, those lineups have rebounded fine, haven't given up a ton of opportunities at the rim, and have yet to be significantly exploited for their lack of size on the perimeter. (It seems that having that French dude back there goes a long way toward bucking conventional wisdom.)

"I understand the narrative around it. I always land on the same reasoning at the very end of it," Johnson told reporters about starting the three guards during the preseason. "They're three of our better players. [...] I think they can dribble, pass, shoot, defend, make winning plays."

Whether they'll continue to do so in this alignment remains to be seen; when offseason signing/veteran power forward Tobias Harris heals up from the calf injury that has cost him the opening month of the season, Johnson will likely have a difficult decision to make. Then again, if the not-so-small-ball Spurs keep blowing everybody's doors off in this look … maybe he won't.

"The biggest thing is, I've got handlers all around me — guys that we want to put the ball in their hands and pick-and-roll around me," Wembanyama said. "That makes things easier."

Since opening the season with losses to the Thunder and Rockets, the Spurs have gotten the opportunity to get right with a pretty friendly stretch of road.

They've had three games against the Jazz, two games against the Kings, and matchups with the Suns and Bulls. They've also caught the Timberwolves without Anthony Edwards or Rudy Gobert, the Mavericks without Cooper Flagg or Kyrie Irving, the Trail Blazers without Ja Morant, and the Warriors right after Stephen Curry went down.

Add it all up, and the Spurs' season-opening slate ranks among the softest in the NBA, according to multiple strength-of-schedule metrics. San Antonio doesn't have to apologize for that, of course. You play the teams that are in front of you, with whoever's in uniform for them when you show up. We've seen plenty of teams fail to capitalize on the opportunity to take down a team at less than full strength. (Looking at you, Mavs.)

Besides, when presented with matchups against shorthanded or sorry opposition, the Spurs have done what good teams are supposed to do: namely, win all of those games, with most of those victories coming by double digits. It's just something to keep an eye on, particularly with a tough opponent like Detroit up next … before three more against the Clippers, Pelicans and Kings. (The games to really keep an eye on? Nov. 27 against Luka and the Lakers, and Nov. 29 against that damn good Celtics team.)

Fox got raked over the coals this summer for his underperformance in the NBA Finals. (Which, it's worth remembering, came with him nursing a high ankle sprain that likely would've sidelined him for weeks had it been the regular season.) His struggles under the brightest lights, combined with Harper's stellar showing in his maiden postseason voyage, primed the pump for the shift to the three-guard lineup, and led to no shortage of calls for the veteran triggerman to cede the stage — and his spot in the Spurs' pecking order — to the rising sophomore.

Well, Fox is still here … and he's kind of threading the needle.

Fox has taken a clear step back in the Spurs' offensive hierarchy, averaging fewer minutes and points per game than he has since his rookie season, and posting far and away the lowest usage rate, time of possession and average number of touches of his 10-year career. He's taking seven fewer field goal attempts per 100 possessions than he did last year.

When Fox shares the floor with Harper and Castle, he's finishing just 15.7% of San Antonio's offensive possessions with a shot attempt, foul drawn or turnover — the kind of usage rate more commonly associated with a complementary player like Isaiah Hartenstein, Toumani Camara or Christian Braun than with a two-time All-Star who has averaged 21 points per game in his career. He's performing well in that reduced role, though.

Fox more frequently takes high-percentage shots — 32% of his attempts are coming at the rim, his highest rate in six years, while corner 3s account for 12% of his shots, a career high — and has almost totally excised long 2-pointers from his repertoire. That, in turn, has led to more efficient scoring. He enters Tuesday's action toting a .551 effective field goal percentage (which accounts for 3-pointers being worth more than 2-pointers) and a .621 true shooting percentage (which factors in 2-point, 3-point and free-throw accuracy) — both of which are by far the highest marks of his career.

He has paired that leaner shot diet with a voracious appetite for freebies, too, drawing shooting fouls on more than 19% of his field-goal attempts — again, a career high. In fact, among 153 players who have logged at least 300 minutes this season, Fox's .496 free-throw attempt rate is the 11th-highest in the NBA, just ahead of free-throw-magnet big men Joel Embiid and Giannis Antetokounmpo.

Pair that just-the-facts-ma'am scoring game with his ongoing facilitation — a team-high 5.8 assists per game, including 33 to Wemby, making them the third-most-frequent assist combo in the NBA thus far — and Fox has managed to continue providing value while still making room for the evolution of the young talent around him. Not bad.

For fans of a certain age, thinking of the Spurs might evoke images of patient, methodical offensive possessions — all entry passes to Tim Duncan on the low block or swing-swing symphonies of "good-to-great" ball movement, where the pace of their play is secondary to the precision of their movements. These aren't your daddy's Spurs, though; these dudes have a predilection toward moving fast and breaking things. Like, for example, you.

In terms of overall pace of play, the Spurs rank just 24th in the NBA, with their average game featuring about 98.8 possessions, according to NBA Advanced Stats. That's due in large part to their defense, though; San Antonio has the longest average defensive possession in the NBA this season, according to PBP Stats. That stands to reason: When you've got to try to figure out how to crowbar open a crevice to attack with Wembanyama menacing along the back line while the likes of Castle and Harper pressure the ball up top, it might take you a little while.

Once the Spurs get the ball, though, they hit the gas. San Antonio has the fifth-fastest average offensive possession this season, and is tied for the fifth-quickest average time to shot, according to Inpredictable.

As noted by the great John Schuhmann, the Spurs lead the NBA in both transition points scored and allowed per game, outscoring opponents by 13 points a night on the break — a massive gap. With their ability to force turnovers, finish possessions with rebounds and advance the ball to all those jet-fueled guards, more than 19% of their offensive possessions come in transition, according to Cleaning the Glass; that's the most in the league. But thanks to their ability to protect the defensive glass, take care of the ball and sprint back, fewer than 12% of their defensive possessions come in transition … which is also No. 1 in the league.

Since at least the start of the 2003-04 season, no team has ever finished the year No. 1 in the league at both generating and preventing transition opportunities. Just another way these Spurs are on pace (ha!) to dispense with conventional wisdom.

Winning the possession battle isn't exactly a revolutionary concept; coaches have been preaching since time immemorial the importance of taking care of the ball on offense and finishing possessions on defense. It's become an increasingly large point of emphasis in recent years, though, as front offices and coaching staffs engaged in the perpetual pursuit of optimization, hunting for every advantage on both ends of the floor. As it turns out, "making sure we get more chances to shoot and score than the other guys" is a pretty solid way to approach things!

The Spurs were pretty good at that last season. When you factor in how many offensive rebounds they pulled in against how many they gave up, how many free-throw attempts they racked up versus how many they conceded, and how many times they turned the ball over compared with how many times they forced opponents to cough it up, San Antonio generated 2.2 more offensive possessions than its opponents per game during the regular season — a good number, but not quite in the upper echelons of the league, like the team that knocked them off in the Finals.

This season, though, the Spurs have tightened the screws virtually across the board. They're grabbing an even higher share of their own missed shots, turning the ball over at the NBA's lowest rate and getting to the free-throw line more frequently, while also forcing more turnovers and curbing their foul rate. Add it up, and through 14 games, San Antonio is generating 7.2 more possessions per game than its opponents — a massive leap over last season, and the No. 1 mark in the league thus far.

Make sure you take more shots on goal than the other guys and you've got a great shot at winning a lot more often than you lose — especially when you put the ball in the hands of dudes who can make those shots. Dudes like …

… the 20-year-old sophomore, whose combination of throwback cool and downhill explosiveness just keeps leaping off the screen pretty much every time you see him.

Harper has responded to his elevation into the starting lineup by continuing to ball out. His usage, free-throw attempt rate and scoring efficiency are all up; his turnover rate is down. Some of that, obviously, comes from more frequently sharing the floor with multiple other high-level ball-handlers, but still: pretty nice to have the guy who's second on your team in touches only coughing it up 8% of the time, one of the lowest rates of any guard in the league.

Coming off that sensational rookie season, Harper is pretty firmly establishing himself as one of the best players in the league when it comes to getting into the paint and finishing on the interior, shooting a frankly bananas 78.6% in the restricted area — better than high-efficiency bigs like Domantas Sabonis, Jarrett Allen, Walker Kessler and Giannis Antetokounmpo. Decent company for a 6-foot-5 sophomore.

Through 14 games, Harper is averaging 18.3 points, 5.5 rebounds, 4.7 assists and 1.0 steals in 31.5 minutes per game, shooting 55% inside the arc, 34% from long distance and 87% from the charity stripe. The list of players to average 18-4-4 for a full season by their age-20 season is just six names long: Magic Johnson, LeBron James, Tyreke Evans, Dončić, LaMelo Ball and Cooper Flagg. Of that group, only Magic shot at least 50% from the field, as Harper is right now.

There's a 7-foot-4 caveat to that, of course. With the exception of rookie Magic playing alongside MVP Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, those other preternatural playmakers didn't have a gravitational force like Wemby pulling attention away from them and opening up opportunities to slash, score and create. But opportunities only matter insofar as you can capitalize on them, and Harper continues to make the most out of his privileged position, perpetually pressuring the defense and looking for all the world like an All-Star in the making — sooner rather than later.

It's not all beer and Skittles in San Antonio, though. As lights-out as the new-look starting lineup has been, moving Harper into the first five has led to some predictable ripple effects in the second unit. The Spurs' reserve corps has a negative net rating for the season, and has — as you might expect — been significantly worse in the minutes when Luke Kornet checks in for Wembanyama, struggling to score even with at least one of Fox, Castle or Harper on the floor.

Some of those struggles might be mitigated by the introduction of Harris, whom the Spurs signed to a two-year, $31 million contract in free agency, and who's yet to play after suffering a left calf strain during training camp. But Johnson will have to shuffle his rotation to make space for the veteran forward. What impact might that have on the minutes and productivity of the likes of Julian Champagnie, whose quick-trigger 3-point shooting has proven so vital for this iteration of the Spurs? Or Keldon Johnson, the reigning Sixth Man of the Year? Or Harrison Barnes, one of just two Spurs (along with Champagnie) knocking down at least 40% of his triples? Or second-year power forward Carter Bryant, whose ongoing development is a clear priority for San Antonio's future plans?

If Harris isn't able to hit the ground running — specifically by spacing the floor at a high level, as he did for Cade Cunningham in Detroit last season — it could throw some sand into the gears of a Spurs offense that has been humming despite not generating a ton from beyond the arc. San Antonio ranks just 26th in the league in the share of its shots that come from beyond the arc, and right around league-average in 3-point shooting accuracy.

That lagging 3-point attempt rate clearly hasn't hampered the offense's effectiveness thus far; some of it stems from the fact that the Spurs hammer the rim as frequently and effectively as nearly any team in the league. After finishing above league-average in 3-point attempt rate the last three seasons, though, you'd imagine Johnson would prefer to find ways to get a few more up — if for no other reason than to be prepared to have multiple styles to play when the postseason comes around and defensive game plans prioritize clogging the paint to take away Wembanyama's rolls to the rim and the three-headed guard monster's driving lanes to the basket.

If opposing defenses are able to congest the interior and force the Spurs to sing for their supper from outside, it'll bring to the forefront perhaps the most significant issue carrying over from last season: whether they have enough bankable 3-point shooting.

As it stands, the entire starting five — Wembanyama, Fox, Castle, Harper and Vassell — currently sits below 35% from deep for the season. Reigning Sixth Man of the Year Johnson is at 22.7%; erstwhile unicorn-turned-indoor cat Kornet hasn't attempted a long ball since April of 2025. The Spurs can make a lot of magic happen in tight confines, thanks to the size and brilliance of Wembanyama, and the speed, skill and force of their guards. If hardly anybody in the rotation can make a jumper when they need to, though, problems will arise.

Those issues — the bench minutes, the shooting, etc. — are not exactly surprises to the Spurs. They're well aware that they've got room to improve.

"If we felt like we're the best version of ourselves at this point in the season, we'll be in trouble," Keldon Johnson recently told reporters.

As excellent as their offense has been, the Spurs have actually underperformed their expected shot quality by the league's fifth-largest amount, according to Synergy Sports Technology's tracking — and all four teams above them in that ranking (Charlotte, Milwaukee, Utah, Chicago) have been terrible, without the talent to meaningfully bridge the gap between expectation and reality. That's certainly not the case in San Antonio.

If Wembanyama (35% from deep last season, 32% this season), Vassell (38% last season, 31% this season) and Johnson (36% last season, 23% this season) tick up toward their previous marks, and any of Fox, Harper or Castle nudge up toward league-average on their looks, this Spurs offense might have another level to reach. Honestly, it might even if only one of those guys keeps the arrow pointed north. You can probably guess which one.

"I can still do more," Wembanyama told reporters after his 52-point masterpiece against the Mavericks. "I mean, my shots weren't falling tonight and that forces me into a style of play that is exhausting. I can do it. I mean, the proof is here. I can do it, but I can be much better if my shots fall. I know it's just a matter of time."

If that happens — if the shots start to fall, the offense soars and the defense remains elite — then it might just be a matter of time before the Spurs are hoisting the Larry O'Brien Championship Trophy, too.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2870. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2867. It’s becoming increasingly clear why LeBron James signed with the Sixers

When LeBron James chose to sign with the Sixers this past offseason, jaws largely dropped around the league.

He chose the Sixers over a final homecoming in Cleveland? Over a reunion in Miami where he could team up with Giannis Antetokounmpo, Bam Adebayo and Simone Fontecchio?! Over a Bay Area team-up with Stephen Curry and Draymond Green? Over joining Anthony Edwards and LaMelo Ball on the Timberwolves?

Fifteen games into the season, the Sixers now sit atop the East at 12-3. They have the second-best record in the NBA, trailing only the San Antonio Spurs and Los Angeles Lakers, both of whom are 12-2. It's by far the best start LeBron has ever gotten off to with a new team.

And they're doing that with less scoring than ever by The King.

Prior to this year, LeBron had never averaged fewer than 20.9 points per game across an entire season. Through 11 games, he's averaging only 15.1 points while shooting 44.3 percent overall (his worst mark since his rookie campaign) and a career-low 22.5 percent from deep.

On Monday, ESPN's Dave McMenamin reported that "James has asked coaches to be patient with him as he expects to find his rhythm and get his conditioning in line by December as he ramps up." (LeBron quickly denied that on Instagram.) While social media naturally ran wild with that excerpt, what came next was arguably more telling.

"One Western Conference executive told ESPN that James' dip in production is evidence that he knew what he was doing when he picked Philly in free agency in July," McMenamin wrote.

That's what got lost in the debates over LeBron's best landing spots over the summer. Everyone seemingly assumed that he wanted to continue averaging 25-plus points per game while being an offensive focal point of his team. But while LeBron has done a remarkably admirable job fending off Father Time, the man turns 42 in December. And Father Time does remain undefeated in sports, even if he occasionally takes longer than expected to claim his victims.

Last year, the Lakers hit their late-season stride when LeBron shifted into more of a tertiary role. In mid-March, Lakers head coach J.J. Redick outright said "the best thing for our team is [LeBron] being the third-highest-used player." And after the season, LeBron expressed appreciation for how he embraced that new challenge.

"I was put into some positions I never played in my career … actually, in my life," he told reporters. "I've never been a third option in my life. So, to be able to thrive in that role for that period of time and then have to step back into the role that I've been accustomed with over my career … and being able to thrive … that was pretty cool for me at this stage in my career."

Granted, with Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves sidelined in last year's playoffs, LeBron showed against the Houston Rockets that he's still able to conjure his usual magic at times. There will inevitably be games this year where the Sixers ask him to take on more of a leading role, too. Their NBA Cup win over the New York Knicks was a good example of that.

But aside from Tyrese Maxey's megawatt smile luring him in, LeBron might have chosen to sign with the Sixers specifically because he didn't want to shoulder the load every night anymore.

In Miami, he would have been teaming up with a nightly 30-point threat in Giannis. But Bam isn't exactly a world-beater on offense, and the Heat didn't have much in the way of reliable offensive depth behind those two. LeBron likely would have been their second-best offensive weapon most nights, and the floor spacing with him, Giannis and Bam all on the floor together would have been even more clunky than the Sixers' starting five.

The same goes in Cleveland, where Donovan Mitchell would have been the clear lead banana had LeBron signed there. But James Harden has long been most effective with the ball in his hands and a relative non-factor off the ball. Evan Mobley still hasn't taken the offensive leap that he was expected to take a few years ago at this point, while Jarrett Allen isn't much of a threat outside of the paint. At worst, LeBron would have been the Cavs' third-best offensive weapon.

While LeBron is the NBA's all-time leading scorer, he's always been more of a playmaker at heart. Philly gave him the best chance to tap into that part of his game, as Maxey, Jaylen Brown, Joel Embiid and VJ Edgecombe all have the potential to take over and bury an opponent. Throw in shooters like Anfernee Simons and Dean Wade off the bench, and he might have been willing to overlook their flimsy frontcourt depth for the time being.

LeBron's shooting percentages figure to regress positively to the mean as the season wears on, but it's also possible that all of those years of being a runaway freight train to the rim have finally taken their toll. He isn't finishing at the basket as explosively as he has for the grand majority of his career. While that might have been a death knell on another team, the Sixers have the high-end talent to make up for it.

So, yes, LeBron might not have had the same type of emotional attachment to Philly than he did to Cleveland or Miami this summer. But from a pure basketball perspective—especially factoring in his potentially diminished scoring prowess at this age—it's becoming increasingly difficult to argue that he made the wrong choice.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2864. Kings are 1-12 and coaching rumors have begun. Two familiar names are surfacing.

The Sacramento Kings are off to a 1-12 start that has started heating up the coach's rumor mill.

The Kings are led by second-year head coach Doug Christie, who took over the position in December 2024 after Sacramento fired Mike Brown. After Christie lead Sacramento to a 27-24 record, the Kings removed the interim tag in April 2025.

Since then, Christie has a 23-72 record, leading to speculation of an eventual coaching change.

According to NBA insider Marc Stein, there are already rumbles in league coaching circles that have named both Tom Thibodeau and Mark Jackson as "potential down-the-road" head coaching candidates for the Kings, if Sacramento eventually makes a coaching change.

More: Kings head coach Doug Christie gets brutally honest about job security

Both candidates would be intriguing, however, the Kings have indicated they have no plans to move on, as they are hesitant to fire Christie, according to The Athletic's Sam Amick.

Thibodeau is a two-time NBA Coach of the Year in 2011 and 2021 and is known for coaching defensive-focused teams that had perennial regular-season success and playoff appearances with the Chicago Bulls, Minnesota Timberwolves and New York Knicks. The Kings have a dynamic point guard in Darius Acuff Jr. and Thibodeau is no stranger to talented point guards having coached Derrick Rose and Jalen Brunson.

Jackson coached the Golden State Warriors for three seasons from 2011 to 2014. He hasn't coached since but has often been credited with helping build the foundation of the future Warriors dynasty led by Stephen Curry, Klay Thompson and Draymond Green. When the Warriors were an afterthought in the league, Jackson's coaching, eye for talent and how to maximize it helped the Warriors become a playoff team before they ascended into a championship team under Steve Kerr.

Christie addressed hot seat rumors after the Kings' Nov. 13 loss to the Portland Trail Blazers.

"I think this seat is hot always, so it doesn't really matter," Christie told USA TODAY. "Obviously, we want to win games. Nobody wants to win more than me, from the standpoint that the record doesn't go on the player's numbers. The record goes on coach's number."

Christie is in the final year of a guaranteed contract. He signed a three-year deal for approximately $2 million a year with the final year (2027-28) a team option.

Sacramento is Christie's first position as a head coach in the NBA. He was the Kings' assistant coach from 2021 to 2024, including the original "Beam Team" that ended a 16-year playoff drought in 2023.

Christie played for seven teams in a 15-season NBA career, including as a starter and defensive enforcer with the Kings during their best era in the early 2000s. Christie made the NBA All-Defensive First Team once and the NBA All-Defensive Second Team three times during his Kings tenure between 2000 and 2005.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: Kings are 1-12 and coaching rumors have begun. Two familiar names are surfacing.

#2857. Bucks Reacts Survey: If you could solve one Bucks problem, what would it be?

Welcome to SB Nation Reacts, a survey of fans across the NBA. Throughout the year we ask questions of the most plugged-in Bucks fans and fans across the country.

Last week's "Who won the Giannis trade?" debate will continue long into the future, but right now it's clear the Milwaukee Bucks are struggling. Big time. After losing to the Atlanta Hawks on Sunday, the Bucks are on a 10-game losing streak, and, at 1-13, sit last in the Eastern Conference standings. Making things worse, they now have sole ownership of the worst record to start a season in franchise history. So, if you could concoct a magic elixir to rid the Bucks of one of their woes, what would it be?

The Bucks are letting it fly from downtown, taking 44.0 threes per game on the season, the second most in the league. Yet, they're hitting them at just a 34.1% clip, the 20th-best mark in the league. And it's not as if the Bucks have a few volume shooters who have gone cold and are dragging the team down. No, outside of Jaime Jaquez Jr. and AJ Green, the slump is across the board. Kel'el Ware (23.7%) and Myles Turner (29.5%) are both shooting career-lows from downtown—Turner's rookie season where he seldom took threes notwithstanding—Tyler Herro is also essentially at a career-low mark (36.1%), and Ryan Rollins is down six percentage points on his career mark (34.4%). In turn, Kasparas Jakučionis, who hit 42.3% of his threes last year, is converting on just 27.5% of his attempts—and that number's buoyed by his 4/10 display against the Atlanta Hawks.

While Milwaukee's raw rebounding numbers look promising—they currently average the eighth-most rebounds per game—that total is deceptive. In fact, advanced metrics paint a very different picture. The Bucks rank 28th in the league in both total rebounding percentage (47.4%) and defensive rebounding percentage (64.6%). Furthermore, despite averaging the sixth-most offensive rebounds per game (13.6), they place a mediocre-at-best 18th in offensive rebounding percentage (30.5%). The discrepancy, then, is further testament to the Bucks' poor shooting: they grab more offensive rebounds because they frequently miss and therefore can, not because of any actual excellence in that area.

However, none of this should really come as a surprise. After all, the Bucks' best rebounder, Ware, is struggling to stay on the court, playing a career-low 18.2 minutes per game, and ranks 43rd in the league in total rebounding percentage (15.7%). Their second best is a rookie point guard. That's not hyperbole, either—Burries ranks second on the team in rebounding percentage at 10.8%. Rollins, another point guard, ranks fourth (9.2%). Outside of a magic elixir, there's no easy fix; these Bucks simply lack strong rebounders—especially in the forward positions—and I will continue to preach it until it's addressed.

Completing the unholy trinity of team flaws, Milwaukee ranks 25th in the league in both turnovers per game (16.9) and turnover percentage (16.7%). Per Cleaning the Glass, Jakučionis ranks in the seventh percentile in the league, relative to position, in turnover percentage. But he's not alone. Kuzma's in the 10th percentile, Turner's in the 12th, Rollins is sinning too (in the 27th percentile), and Jaquez is in the 31st. In fact, the only Buck to rank in the upper echelon for their position is the seldom used—and now injured—Ousmane Dieng. Considering this, there's no wonder why the Bucks are struggling to score; not only do they lack a legitimate go-to scorer (sorry, Tyler), they also lack top-level playmaking (yes, ball security is an essential component to that).

So, long-range shooting, controlling the glass, or taking care of the ball, which of these is your elixir targeting? Alternatively, is there something else atop your priority list?

Let us know your thoughts by voting below—and don't forget to explain your reasons in the comments.

#2850. From the injury bug to youth movements, here are notes on all 30 teams to help you in fantasy basketball in Week 5

A quick fantasy basketball breakdown on all 30 teams after Week 5 of the 2026-27 NBA season. Here are five fantasy trends grabbing my attention coming out of Week 4, plus rapid-fire notes on every other team.

Warriors: Stephen Curry's oblique tear will keep him out at least two weeks, and the team is bracing for an absence closer to a month. Golden State got blown out 123-85 by the Spurs in the first game without him. De'Anthony Melton got the first crack at the starting spot and is the priority add. However, this team will lean more on Yaxel Lendeborg and Brandin Podziemski until Curry returns.

Jazz: Lauri Markkanen is out at least a month with a fractured shooting hand. Keyonte George (ankle) is back and should see hefty usage. Darryn Peterson just snapped a five-game streak of scoring 21+ points across Weeks 3 and 4. He continues to trend up at 4.5 assists per game in November with nearly a steal and a block.

Hornets: Brandon Miller returned and should immediately reclaim a heavy-usage role. Coby White has played six games under a 20-minute cap, but he's expected to ramp up soon — he's dropped at least 20 points in his last two outings. Don't expect to see Kon Knueppel for at least a month after aggravating his hamstring.

Grizzlies: Memphis is 9-4 without Ty Jerome because the kids keep delivering. Cedric Coward ranks 58th in points per game and 25th in 9-cat through Monday. Cam Spencer continues to thrive in a starting role, averaging 13 points, 4 boards, 10 assists and 1 steal per game with Jerome out. Don't drop him.

Timberwolves: Minnesota is surprisingly 7-3 without Anthony Edwards. Ayo Dosunmu is balling, putting up 20-5-3 on 53/42/96 shooting. You can't beat that efficiency. Meanwhile, Terrence Shannon Jr. just dropped a career-high 34 on Portland on Monday night. LaMelo Ball is in a shooting funk, but I'm buying low if a manager is rattled.

Pistons: Jalen Duren is out at least two more weeks. Daniss Jenkins' minutes are up over the last four games, averaging 36 minutes, which has led to a productive 20-4-4 line over that span. Keep holding, especially now that he's starting.

Cavaliers: With all five starters resting Saturday, Tyrese Proctor posted one of the most random triple-doubles of the season (24-11-10 ) against Dallas. I was reluctant to hold, but he started in Monday's win over the Bulls, with Evan Mobley (toe) out of the lineup. Mobley will be re-evaluated in 7-10 days. Meleek Thomas and Mario Hezonja are deep-league names to watch.

Magic: Franz Wagner is fifth in the NBA in assists and has strung together four straight games of at least 30 points across Weeks 3 and 4. Point Franz is crushing his ADP, and it'll be interesting to see what this looks like when Paolo Banchero returns.

Pacers: With Tyrese Haliburton still out, Andrew Nembhard's done a great job facilitating. His 7.6 assists per game are seventh-best in the NBA. Nembhard missed Sunday's clash with Philly, but the Pacers don't play again until Wednesday. A few days off should help him get back out there.

Heat: Did you know that Davion Mitchell is leading the league in assists? Let that sink in. Dude's been unreal to start the season. Bam Adebayo left Monday's win over Houston with hip tightness, though Miami doesn't expect a long-term issue. If he sits, Mitchell gets a bump.

Hawks:  Sell Jalen Johnson. He posted his first triple-double (27-11-10) of the season a couple of games ago, and looks back to normal following a three-game absence due to an ankle sprain. Since 2022-23 (excluding a low-usage rookie year), he's missed 28% of regular-season games, including a season-ending torn labrum in 2025.

Pelicans: Dejounte Murray wants out, and per Marc Stein, the Pelicans feel the same. But a deal isn't close with his age, Achilles history and nearly $33 million salary. He's away from the team, so cut him in shallow leagues. Jeremiah Fears' stock is way up.

Wizards: Trae Young went scoreless Sunday for the first time in his career as the Wizards' losing streak hit eight games on Monday. I don't think he'll keep shooting this bad, and at least he's still getting the assists and points. There's a case to sell high on AD, but I wouldn't yet. Seems like he's auditioning for a trade …

Raptors: There's a small window to buy Kawhi Leonard if you're willing to take on some risk. He's still very efficient and productive when he plays.

Suns: Sell Miles Bridges; he's not a top-35 player in 9-cat. In fact, the slide is already happening, as he was top-25 last week. But also, I'd drop Jalen Green — still 67% rostered, but the team is operating better with players like Collin Gillespie, Jordan Goodwin and Luke Kennard taking his minutes.

76ers: LeBron James passed Jason Kidd for third all-time in assists, then sat Monday while Joel Embiid dropped a season-high 37 to move Philly to 12-3. That's one of the best records in the NBA, and the Sixers have done it with James still not fully ramped up, per ESPN's Dave McMenamin. If this is the Sixers before LeBron hits full speed, the ceiling is scary. Now, they need to fortify that frontcourt and add some shooters by the deadline to really make a run for it.

Celtics: Boston is 11-2 after a 131-94 demolition of Dallas in which it led 42-9 after one quarter. Jayson Tatum is the engine, dishing out a career-best 7.1 assists per game. And he's still top-10 in scoring.

Spurs: San Antonio has won 12 straight. I like the Spurs schedule this week, so pick up guys like Julian Champagnie and Keldon Johnson. Hopefully De'Aaron Fox returns from his hip injury and we'll get to see Tobias Harris make his season debut at some point this week.

Lakers: The 11-2 Lakers keep winning shorthanded because Luka Dončić has been unconscious, gunning for his first MVP.  He's the top player in points leagues and second in 9-cat, averaging 39-7-8 with 49/40/87 shooting splits. Austin Reaves (heel bruise) and Walker Kessler (lower leg soreness) are both out Tuesday, but Reaves could return later this week. Quentin Grimes is a deep-league add with Reaves out.

Rockets: KD's taken his foot off the gas a bit, which has opened up Alperen Şengün's game. Şengün is in the top-10 in assists and over his last six games, he has averaged 23 points, 10 rebounds and 6 assists with 1.7 stocks, shooting 58% from the field.

Bucks: Brayden Burries and Ryan Rollins are still battling at point guard. Burries is starting, but he hasn't separated. I understand if you don't want to roster either of them right now. I still have Burries in a couple spots because, with the season going the way it is, why not give the keys to your first-round pick?

Bulls: Chicago lost prized rookie Caleb Wilson for 4-6 weeks with a fractured hand. Matas Buzelis' numbers are up pretty much across the board in the three games Wilson's been out. Coincidence? I think not. I still say sell high the next time Josh Giddey goes off.

Clippers: Brandon Ingram could debut before the road trip ends Nov. 25, and they need him.

Kings: At 1-12 with Doug Christie on the hot seat, rookie Darius Acuff Jr. keeps a massive usage role no matter who's coaching.

Knicks: The defending champs are 7-6, and Josh Hart (two straight scoreless games) admits he's "not in a good headspace." Mikal Bridges is still going through it, too.

Mavericks: It's a lot to ask of a teenager, but Cooper Flagg is making it happen. He's a top-10 player in points and 9-cat through Week 4.

Nets: The Day'Ron Sharpe panic should die down now. He's been a top-35 player in 9-cat over the past two weeks and was top-60 in High Score in Week 4. Let the good times roll.

Nuggets: Jamal Murray and Nikola Jokić are the first duo since 1997-98 to log 1,000 assists to each other. Nobody has figured out how to stop their dribble handoff and pick-and-roll yet.

Thunder: It takes a village and though SGA's efficiency is suffering, he's putting the team on his back. I'd try to buy some Ajay Mitchell stock.

Trail Blazers: Who would've thought the Blazers would be 9-4 right now? With Ja Morant out with a strained hamstring, Deni Avdija has been a first-round asset. He's elevated his efficiency and scoring to 25-6-6 with 51/40/75 shooting splits without Ja on the floor.

#2846. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2843. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2842. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

The Warriors were on the wrong side of history Monday night.

Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors reacts during the fourth quarter of the NBA game against the New Orleans Pelicans at Smoothie King Center on Nov. 16, 2026 in New Orleans, Louisiana. Getty Images

#2834. Arch Manning Commits To Senior Day While Draft Future Lingers

Arch Manning is taking his senior year experience to the fullest, but he refuses to lock in his NFL future just yet. The Texas quarterback confirmed Monday that he will participate in Senior Day festivities before the team's final home game against Arkansas on Saturday.

The pressure to leave early is real, but Manning insists his mind is on the present. Texas sits at 8-2 and is on a four-game win streak after a shaky start. The team has already beaten ranked opponents like Ole Miss and LSU, proving they can hang with the best. Manning has thrown 19 touchdowns and 10 interceptions while completing 64.7 percent of his passes. He told reporters that his career has "been great" as the team looks to march to a championship. "I feel like I've grown a lot as a person and a player," Manning said.

Participating in the pregame ceremony does not mean Manning is done with college football. He could easily return to Austin next season if he decides against the 2027 NFL Draft. The Longhorns might even host a College Football Playoff game, which would give him one last shot at glory on the biggest stage. Manning insists he is not looking past the upcoming matchup. "I've enjoyed my time, but I'm just focused on this week, winning this week. I'm not really thinking about the future right now," he said, according to the New York Post.

His grandfather Archie Manning and uncles Peyton and Eli all became NFL legends, adding weight to every decision he makes. Texas fans want to know if he will stay or go, but the quarterback is keeping his cards close. The team's recent success suggests they might not need him to leave early to compete. Manning's 20-5 record as a starter shows he has already made an impact.

The next game is Saturday night in Austin. The No. 6 ranked Longhorns will face Arkansas in what could be their final home game of the regular season.

#2832. Jake LaRavia is the glue to the Lakers’ starting lineup

With Walker Kessler catching his breath on the bench, Devin Booker saw his opportunity.

Saucers for eyes, Booker made a dash to the rim. He caught the pass and went up for what he thought would be his easiest shot of the game.

Trailing him like his own shadow, however, was Jake LaRavia. The forward swatted the layup attempt away and then found Luka Dončić leaking out ahead for a bucket.

On the Suns' next possession, Jalen Green decided to try his luck against the Lakers' small lineup. The result was the same.

Green's drive and shot at the rim were stonewalled by the 6'8" LaRavia who acted as the team's backline protector. On the other end, the Lakers got a dunk.

These were ultimately just two possessions in another Lakers' win. But they also encapsulate why LaRavia has been an invaluable cog to the team's impressive 12-2 start.

After being inserted into the starting lineup earlier in the season due to an injury to Ziaire Williams, LaRavia's play and fit next to the Lakers' stars since has made it difficult to take him out.

While he doesn't have the springiness or shooting diversity that Williams does, LaRavia makes his impact felt by filling in the gaps.

This is because, unlike the other four players in the Lakers' starting lineup, LaRavia isn't elite or even really good at a singular thing. Instead, he helps everything click into place by being solid in multiple areas.

First and foremost, LaRavia plays hard. It's cliché, but there's immense and often overlooked value in having a player who approaches every game with care.

Sometimes his motor can run too hot, and his aggressiveness leads to whistles. LaRavia has been called for 45 fouls this season — fifth-most among all players.

But in the aggregate, his tendency to enthusiastically do all the "little things" that lead to winning is best illustrated by LaRavia's fingerprints being found all over the Lakers' leaderboards.

He currently is second on the team in deflections, second in offensive rebounds, third in blocks and fourth in total rebounds and assists. These stats also display how LaRavia functionally greases the wheels on both ends for the Lakers.

Sturdier and more stout than Williams, LaRavia has helped the Lakers' starters defensively (86th and 90th percentile in block and steal rates) and on the glass.

The team is performing better but is still poor at preventing second chances with LaRavia on, but has compensated for its weakness on one end by making it a strength on the other end. With LaRavia on, the Lakers' starters have an absurd offensive rebound percentage of 37.9% compared to just 18.2% when replaced with Williams.

LaRavia isn't the sole reason for their recent dominance on the offensive glass, but he is a big part of it.

Through his excellent timing on his crashes and knack for playing physical, LaRavia has gobbled up 8.8% of the Lakers' misses this season. That ranks in the 95th percentile among all forwards.

Beyond his work on the glass, LaRavia has also made major strides in his 3-point shooting.

After a disappointing season where LaRavia struggled from the perimeter and admittedly hit a "mental lapse," he's bounced back in a big way thanks to new shooting mechanics and rekindled confidence.

While not the most aesthetically pleasing windup, it's hard to argue with the results. LaRavia is currently shooting a blistering 53% from the corners and 48% overall from beyond the arc this season.

Those numbers will surely come down with a larger sample, but seeing them go in early, hopefully, will encourage him to keep firing throughout the year.

It's early, but this version of LaRavia on this version of the Lakers has been humming.

The starting five with LaRavia in it is outscoring their opponents by 28.3 points per 100 possessions — the second-best lineup in the NBA — and is holding teams to a defensive rating of just 93.2.

For context, not only would that be the best defense in the league, but it would be 12 points better than the current league leader.

LaRavia's game is an acquired taste. We're not talking caviar. It's more like Brussels sprouts. Hearty, versatile and kinda weird sometimes.

He'll frustratingly still pass up a shot or make an ill-advised drive. But he'll also be the first on the floor for a loose ball and in the right spot for a rotation.

He's nowhere near a perfect player, but LaRavia may be the perfect fit on this team especially at his price point.

After the Lakers were unable to land a certified starting four to replace the gargantuan hole left by LeBron James over the offseason, they headed into the year hoping to fill the void by committee. And they have so far.

To be clear, LaRavia isn't James. Nor is he even likely the long-term answer to the Lakers' starting forward position.

However, he has provided a blueprint for the type of player who works well next to the Lakers' trio of the future. He and the Lakers should chalk that up as a win. Even if he fouls a little too much.

All stats courtesy of Cleaning the Glass unless otherwise stated. You can follow Alex on Bluesky at @alexregla.bsky.social.

#2827. Ryan Conwell Proves Heat Found Gold In Second-Round Pick

Ryan Conwell just dropped 20 points on the Cleveland Cavaliers, and that performance changes the whole conversation about Miami's backcourt. This rookie is not just filling time; he is making plays when the team needs them most. Sun Sentinel's Ira Winderman notes that Conwell has emerged as a valuable second-round selection for the franchise.

The numbers back up the hype from Friday night. Conwell finished with five rebounds while shooting 6-for-12 from the field and 4-for-9 from deep against the Cavs, per All U Can Heat. His last four games show a player on the rise, averaging 11 points and hitting 40 percent of his threes. The Heat went 3-1 during this specific stretch, proving the rookie's impact on the win column.

Heat fans know how thin the roster gets when injuries pile up. Pelle Larsson and Klay Thompson sat out the Friday night victory, leaving a massive hole in the lineup. Conwell stepped in and logged 33 minutes, playing more than any Heat player aside from Bam Adebayo and Davion Mitchell, according to All U Can Heat. That heavy workload shows exactly how much the coaching staff trusts him right now.

But there is a clear difference between Conwell and other options. Sun Sentinel reporter Ira Winderman noted that comparing Conwell to Kasparas Jakucionis is somewhat apples to oranges. Jakucionis is more of a point guard, while Conwell operates as an undersized scorer who can create his own shot. This distinction matters because Miami needs someone who can score when the defense collapses.

Even with a strong showing, the team faces a tough reality about its backcourt. Ira Winderman highlighted that insufficient depth at the point guard spot continues to be a major concern for Miami. During Friday's matchup, Erik Spoelstra faced significant difficulty resting Davion Mitchell, while Dru Smith contributed little in his short appearance. The truth is that the Heat likely require point guard depth more than in any other area.

The story of how Conwell landed in Miami adds another layer to his value. Per Sun Sentinel, he resulted from a compensatory second-round pick the Heat obtained from the Hornets because they were not informed about the gambling investigation depth concerning Terry Rozier before the trade. That pick turned into a player who arrived exactly when needed.

Some fans might compare his future value to the first-round pick the Hornets still hold from the Rozier trade, which is top-14 protected in June and otherwise unprotected in 2028, according to reports. That potential future asset highlights just how valuable a current contributor like Conwell can be. A more fitting comparison for Ryan could be whatever asset the Hornets eventually acquire from that first-round selection.
