#2783. Investors look to Nvidia's earnings and Anthropic's IPO to refresh the tech rally

I've spent the past 20 years pushing back against the periodically popular notion that markets are replaying the late-1990s and early-2000s tech bubble and bust.

We've simply never matched the levels of gross overvaluation, pervasive public greed, fevered IPO issuance, corporate malinvestment and Wall Street malfeasance that set up a generational market peak, a halving of the S&P 500 over two-and-a-half years and a lost decade for stocks.

We still haven't. Even if June was the peak in AI speculative fervor, as seems possible, and if Oct. 6 proves to have been a consequential broad-market top, the major indexes simply never stretched far enough into the thin air to make a potential retrenchment nearly as damaging as the early 2000s were.

Still, in a more muted fashion, markets have been behaving in ways that generate uncomfortable comparisons to past buying climaxes accompanying a technology revolution.

There is still room and time for the market to prove that the messy sell-down in AI-reliant growth stocks is simply a painful adjustment phase and the S&P 500's six-month purgatorial range a mid-cycle refresh. But it will need to start getting on with it.

Everyone wants to celebrate the way the market has absorbed the damage to semiconductors and other AI-buildout plays, rendering it a controlled demolition rather than indiscriminate destruction.

The Financial Times Unhedged newsletter on Monday called it "The perfect sell-off," the same day the Wall Street Journal cheered, "When Nvidia Stumbled, Equal-Weight Funds Delivered."

This is true, as far as it goes. A rush into defensive and "quality" stocks has largely offset the drag from Nvidia's $2 trillion market-cap loss from its October peak.

But recent defensive leadership, especially from low-growth consumer staples and Big Pharma, sends a less-reassuring macro message, and those groups lack the earnings momentum, narrative appeal or market-cap heft to carry a bull market to new heights.

BTIG technical strategist Jonathan Krinsky has noted echoes of the 2000 period when "boring" defensive stocks ripped as tech rolled over, supporting the tape for a while. Barclays equity desk noted Monday that the "quality factor" basket of stocks just had one of the best 100-day runs ever of the past 20 years.

And JP Morgan here plots the stampede into "low-volatility" stocks, mirroring the hasty retreat from "high-momentum" areas. There's room here for defensives to get even more popular before tripping multi-year extremes, but is that what an index investor should root for?

As for the ascendance of everyone's favorite, the equal-weighted S&P 500, Ned Davis Research analyst London Stockton this month noted that just over 50% of S&P 500 stocks were outperforming the index itself in 2026. Sounds healthy, right? Only to a point.

He explains: "Levels [of stocks outperforming the index] above 58% have most often occurred in and around years where there has been a large bear market." The current phase follows a period when leadership was extremely narrow, producing a "resemblance of the prior three years (2023-2025) to the two years prior to the last Tech bubble (1998-1999) with a very small percentage of companies outperforming the overall S&P 500.

"It is not likely that we are in a situation like in 2000," Stockton concludes, "but this highlights the importance that these large tech stocks at the very least do not decline substantially for the S&P 500 to continue rallying higher."

There are other straws in the wind that harken to prior tech busts:  The Federal Reserve is trying to restrain the corporate sector from overheating in the latter part of a cycle, same as in 1999. Retail traders have maniacally bought the cascading decline in Nvidia shares the past week or so, invoking the voracious dip-buying made famous in the late-'90s which worked until it failed spectacularly.  The market's refusal to pay up for sizzling earnings growth raises alarms about whether they are pulled forward, unsustainable or overstated; profits from 2000 were later revised down radically.

And Nvidia's alleged breach of chip-export rules and the forced departure of CEO Jensen Huang are a reminder that major corporate scandals were a key feature of the Tech Bubble's prolonged reckoning, though nothing resembling the multiple accounting frauds of that era are implied here.

Again, this is the kind of litany of worry and woe that gets recited each time a tech-propelled bull market breaks stride for a bit; more often than not a recovery follows to clear the gloom.

Perhaps Nvidia's results on Tuesday clearing the way for the company to restart stock buybacks and the final allocation of Anthropic's IPO shares will refresh the tech bid. With luck, the huge "hyperscaler" tech-platform stocks will move back to the fore as well-funded stewards of a more sober AI expansion.

Maybe, after weeks of Wall Street insisting that hedge-fund tech-stock positioning is scraping multi-year lows, this will finally presage an upside pain trade.

The vaunted seasonal, post-election tailwinds that the consensus was expecting to fill market's sails and lift the indexes from the floor of an enervating six-month trading range are running late. They need to arrive quite soon. Otherwise, the soggy tape - along with signs of ebbing liquidity reflected in Bitcoin weakness a strong dollar and jumpier equity volatility - will make those tech-bust echoes louder.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Despite pervasive bearish sentiment and the average stock trading near new highs, our historical studies suggest the market remains vulnerable to disappointment. With defensive leadership still dominating, risk appetite weak, and financial conditions still tight, maintaining a short-term cautious/bearish stance still seems warranted."

-Twenty years ago this week, I wrote a cover story for Barron's that antagonized plenty of friends in the stock-trading profession by foretelling a future of fast, cheap electronic markets that has largely come to pass.

Called "Death of the Floor," the piece detailed the regulatory, technological and behavioral shifts that were ushering in high-frequency, low-touch trading and pressuring the old exchange model.

Reading it now, the conclusions seem utterly obvious and inevitable, though at the time there were still 2,500 NYSE floor brokers and specialists trying to remain in the middle of most trades.

"For the specialists who manage trading in individual stocks and for the floor brokers who depend on the in-person auction model, a tough challenge is becoming even tougher. To stay in the game, they must master cybertrading. For the small investor, this is almost entirely good news. Trading costs should continue to fall, while the likelihood of getting the best price should improve."

Happily, exchanges have adapted and evolved – to zero commissions, thousands of ETFs and microsecond executions - and we have a market that can handle multiples of the capital flow that was routine back then.

If I have one regret about the piece, it's that my good friend and former colleague Bob Pisani thought the CNBC reporter skeleton illustrating the article was meant to depict him.

I assured him, many times, that it was not.

-Almost every business relies heavily on its most loyal repeat customers. But this skew seems extreme for online-betting companies.

A Bloomberg piece picked up here by Yahoo Finance reports that one-third of all sports bettors produces some 97% of revenue at digital sportsbooks. It says the 1% most active gamblers on average lost 78 cents of each dollar bet, twice the loss rate of the norm among all customers.

One can view this as simply paying for entertainment (I "lose" 100 cents of every dollar I place on a movie ticket), but the social disapproval toward the industry seems to have grown.

Add it to the swirl of issues surrounding the legal and de-facto decriminalized wagering industry.

Perceived hostility of Congressional Democrats to prediction-markets venues taking sports bets in states that haven't explicitly approved sports gambling is seen as a plus for DraftKings and Flutter.

Meantime, Robinhood has blurred the lines between investing and gambling by aggressively expanding into prediction markets. And on Monday it was reported that Kalshi has looked to combine investing and betting from the opposite direction, acquiring a brokerage firm in order to offer stock trading to its prediction-markets clientele.

Adults should be free to behave unwisely with their money in search of a windfall. But brokers promoting the negative-sum game of wagering while professing to help the little guy accumulate long-term wealth sends a dissonant message.

The ducks that were quacking loudly ahead of SpaceX's June IPO are much quieter in advance of Anthropic's planned debut this week.

Here is the year-to-date performance of two ETFs that hold shares of recent new listings, Renaissance IPO and First Trust US Equity Opportunities, relative to the S&P 500.

As of June 12, when SpaceX priced, these funds had gone vertical as the public worked itself into an anticipatory lather, the Renaissance fund running ahead of the S&P by some 20 percentage points in the prior six months.

Since then, the IPO funds have deflated (both now have large positions in SpaceX, of course) and there's a bit more focus on whether Anthropic's target valuation is justified given the vast rethink of the AI-proliferation thesis.

All else being equal, a more favorable setup now versus then for a market being asked to swallow a new $1 trillion-plus ticker.

#2782. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2781. Titans put Wan'Dale Robinson on injured reserve

The Titans have been happy with quarterback Cam Ward's recent play, but he'll have to keep it going without his most frequent target in the passing game.

Wide receiver Wan'Dale Robinson has been placed on injured reserve. Robinson injured his ankle during last Sunday's loss to the Jaguars.

#2779. Nvidia warns chip buyers of legal risks tied to China AI access, sources tell CNBC

Nvidia warned its customers that new legislation on Chinese companies remotely accessing its artificial-intelligence chips could expose it and them to U.S. "legal action," sources told CNBC.

The chip giant is facing increasing scrutiny from U.S. lawmakers over Chinese companies' access to its advanced chips, which U.S. export controls restrict from going to China.

Nvidia messaged chip buyers in recent months to caution that the proposed Remote Access Security Act, or RASA, would give the U.S. government broader authority to control access to cloud services in the same way as physical hardware, potentially exposing both parties to legal risk, according to two people with direct knowledge of the matter. The people asked not to be named while discussing private communications.

It also said that, for now, it would not sell its chips to customers who rent more than 50% of the graphics processing units, or GPUs, to Chinese companies, the people said.

Separately, the Financial Times reported earlier this month that Nvidia verbally told data-center clients to direct at least 51% of their services to non-Chinese customers under future contracts.

Regulators have had limited powers to control companies' access to those chips through cloud services hosted overseas.

RASA passed the House of Representatives in January but has not yet passed the Senate. Remote access to the chips has provoked the ire of President Donald Trump's administration, which has sought to keep the chips out of China to maintain the U.S. lead in the AI race.

CNBC reported on Nov. 5 that Chinese lab Moonshot AI sought access to Nvidia chips through data centers in Europe. The Trump administration previously singled the company out, accusing it in July of accessing the chips through centers in East Asia.

The U.S. Commerce Department's Bureau of Industry and Security, or BIS, announced on Monday an interim final rule designed to prevent Chinese companies' remote access to AI chips.

Nvidia and BIS did not respond to a request for comment.

On Sunday, U.S. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent called for a "dramatic shakeup" at Nvidia after Jensen Huang's shock departure as CEO, amid reports of an extramarital affair with a woman U.S. authorities reportedly suspect of spying for China.

Bessent suggested other Nvidia executives should face scrutiny over the chip giant's compliance with export controls.

"I want to keep coming back to what was the culture at Nvidia, and were they pressing these seemingly illicit sales to goose their revenues and their earnings?" Bessent told Fox News.

In an August securities filing, Nvidia said it sought to "strictly comply with all applicable export control regulators" but did not have "physical control of our products after sale" and relied on its customers' and partners' compliance programs.

Huang has been asked to testify before the Senate Banking Committee on Dec. 3.

#2777. Trump has arrived in Asia. Here’s what he said about Iran and Deutsche Bank

U.S. President Donald Trump has arrived for his high-stakes trip to Asia, and took a range of questions on subjects from Iran to Deutsche Bank leaking details about his tax.

The president landed in Manila, Philippines, for the initial leg of his trip on Tuesday and answered rapid-fire questions from the press while meeting with other leaders.

Asked about a possible Iranian threat to Washington, D.C., he said that, if there were an attack, Iran would "pay a price the likes of which no country has ever paid before."

The question was about CNN reporting that U.S. intelligence suggested the country was planning to target senior members of Trump's administration with a dirty bomb or chemical weapon in the U.S. capital.

"We take it very seriously because they happen to be crazy," Trump said. CNBC has not independently verified CNN's report.

But the president also said he believed his administration was "very close to getting it done" when it came to the Iran war.

While Trump was airborne, the Financial Times reported Deutsche Bank had mistakenly leaked details of his taxes in March 2017, during his first presidential term. The mistake was kept from the White House at the time, according to the FT.

In a statement, Deutsche Bank said the issues detailed in Tuesday's media reports go back many years. "Where relevant, we reviewed these matters at the time and worked closely with regulators," the lender said.

Asked about the situation in Manila, the president said, "I paid a lot of tax."

"They shouldn't have done it. It's illegal for them to do it," he added.

Trump was also asked about Nvidia's announcement on Tuesday of a new National Security and Export Control Oversight Committee. The company, which reports earnings later on Tuesday, is reeling from the sudden departure of CEO Jensen Huang, who resigned last week after having an affair with a woman U.S. intelligence reportedly believes to be a Chinese spy.

"It's a great company. It's a great idea," Trump said.

Trump is due to fly to Shenzhen, China, where he is scheduled to meet Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin for a three-way meeting on Thursday, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation summit.

He is also due to hold a separate bilateral meeting with Xi. Asked by a reporter if he would have a separate meeting with only Putin, Trump said: "I don't know, but I would, if it's available, I would do that. He's coming. I mean, it's really with the guests. I am the guest of President Xi."

Trump met the leaders of Australia, India and Japan as part of the Quad grouping in the Philippines and the prime minister of Thailand. He is due to China on Wednesday.

Speaking at a dinner with Southeast Asian leaders as the event wrapped up for the day, Trump touted his record during his second term, citing investment into the U.S., trade deals made with countries represented by attendees at the dinner, energy infrastructure buildouts and America's AI industry.

Trump, who has led a campaign to rename artificial intelligence "super intelligence," or "SI," told leaders around the table that the U.S. is "leading the world" in the technology.

"We have the world's most powerful SI ecosystem," he said. "We're leading everybody by a lot, and we hope to keep it that way, and we will keep it that way."

He added that the U.S. has "the strongest and most capable military on the planet," pointing to investments in the armed forces and the country's defense sector.

"We make the greatest equipment. You saw that in Venezuela. You see it in Iran. You see it everywhere," Trump said.

He didn't need to say a word to convey his feelings in Golden State's 115-94 loss to the Pelicans. His body language came with as much candor as anything to escape his mouth.

Lendeborg poured in a team-high 28 points, but almost all of them came after the Warriors had dug themselves into a 31-point hole to a team that had lost its previous six games.

Golden State Warriors forward Yaxel Lendeborg (1) reacts during the fourth quarter of an NBA game against the New Orleans Pelicans on Monday, Nov. 16, 2026. Getty Images

Golden State Warriors forward Yaxel Lendeborg (1) dribbles against New Orleans Pelicans guard Jeremiah Fears (0) on Monday, Nov. 16, 2026, in New Orleans. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Lendeborg scored 20 on 7-of-14 shooting after halftime, but that was too little, too late.

Golden State Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg (1) plays against New Orleans Pelicans guard Jaron Pierre Jr. (10) in New Orleans, Monday, Nov. 16, 2026. AP Photo/Morgan Simmons

Golden State Warriors forward Draymond Green (23) sits on the bench against the New Orleans Pelicans on Monday, Nov. 16, 2026, in New Orleans. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Internally, at least, the Warriors are in control of the dimmer switch.

#2774. U.S. agencies say they initiated investigation into Nvidia partner Megaspeed in 2024

The U.S. Commerce and Justice Department on Monday stateside said they had initiated an investigation into Singapore-based company Megaspeed in 2024 to "address inaccurate information circulating in public reporting."

Megaspeed International, a cloud computing firm that operates data centers across Southeast Asia, has been drawn into the fallout surrounding the leadership shake-up at chip giant Nvidia.

In a joint release, the departments outlined a timeline of their investigations into Megaspeed, saying the probes began in 2024 over potential export-control violations.

In June 2025, Nvidia placed Megaspeed on its "no sell" list, after Commerce Department investigators requested records from Nvidia concerning direct or indirect sales to the company.

In the same month, U.S. officials and Singapore police conducted an in-person inspection of Megaspeed and interviewed Alice Huang, who was allegedly involved in a relationship with Nvidia co-founder Jensen Huang.

"Following this enforcement action, the Department of Justice took the lead role in the criminal investigation of Megaspeed," the statement said.

On Wednesday, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick also reposted the statement, adding, "The Department of Commerce works to protect the U.S. economy and our critical industries every day. We are very proud of the work of the Bureau of Industry and Security."

In response to the statement, Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council of Foreign Relations wrote on X, "Very, very strange statement tonight... about Megaspeed. The facts here just don't add up, and this does nothing to explain why Commerce did not cut Megaspeed off from chips."McGuire said that Megaspeed kept buying chips after Nvidia put them on their no-trade list, and asked if Nvidia also blacklisted Megaspeed's subsidaries Speedmatrix and PT Megaspeed International. "I would bet they did not! If they kept shipping to them after blacklisting Megaspeed, that would be very concerning," McGuire added.

The statement comes after Singapore's Ministry of Home Affairs said the first time authorities there learned of U.S. interest in Megaspeed and Alice Huang was in June. Singapore had already been conducting its own independent investigation and said she was not investigated for espionage.

The city-state barred Alice Huang from re-entering the country in February 2026 after authorities found that she had knowingly provided inaccurate information about her role at Megaspeed.

Alice Huang was reportedly recruited by China's Ministry of State Security to gather information on Jensen Huang. The Justice Department has not reached a conclusion on whether she was a Chinese spy.

Jensen Huang resigned as CEO last week after revelations about multiple extramarital relationships, including with two Nvidia employees. The tech giant has appointed Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress as interim CEO.

The agencies' statement also comes as Nvidia prepares to report its third-quarter results Tuesday afternoon U.S. time, with Kress set to lead her first earnings call.

Earlier on Monday in the U.S., the Commerce Department moved to close a loophole that has allowed Chinese companies to access Nvidia's advanced computing power through data centers in third countries.

The interim final rule from the department's Bureau of Industry and Security is aimed at restricting remote access to advanced U.S. computing technology.

It also comes amid growing scrutiny of Lutnick for not having done enough to prevent restricted Nvidia chips and computing capacity from reaching Chinese companies.

— CNBC's Anniek Bao and Dan Mangan contributed to this report.

And it kind of just shows how quick in the NFL, if you just keep staying with it and keep rolling with it, how quickly things can turn around. And so I'm proud of our guys for just staying with it."

#2771. LeBron Log No. 5: Popcorn in hand, a viral chorus and ‘basketball AI'

LeBron Log No. 5: Popcorn in hand, a viral chorus and 'basketball AI' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James spent Monday night in street clothes.

He still managed to argue a call with popcorn in his hand, practice his golf swing during a timeout and reject a report about himself before the Sixers left Brooklyn with a win.

That followed a Sunday afternoon in which James passed Jason Kidd on the NBA's career assists list and received an unusual description from Pacers head coach Rick Carlisle.

Welcome to the fifth edition of the LeBron Log.

James sat out the Sixers' 135-123 win over the Nets because of low back and sciatica management.

It was his fourth absence through 15 games. James has yet to play both ends of a back-to-back this season after logging nearly 33 minutes Sunday against Indiana.

He watched Monday's game from the end of the Sixers' bench in a yellow hat and an "Ohio Baby" sweatshirt. James snacked from a small bag before switching to popcorn.

When Joel Embiid believed he was fouled on a first-half drive, James stood and pleaded his case to official Jacyn Goble without putting down the box. He also joined conversations between teammates and officials during several timeouts.

James found another way to pass a third-quarter stoppage. As "Hot To Go" played inside Barclays Center, he practiced his golf swing near the bench.

His most pointed contribution came on Instagram.

An ESPN story by Dave McMenamin cited a Sixers source who said James had asked the coaching staff to remain patient while he worked toward finding his rhythm and conditioning by December.

James reposted ESPN's graphic and rejected the account.

"🧢! Those words never came out of my mouth!" he wrote.

The Sixers improved to 4-0 without James. They have beaten the Bucks, Hornets, Raptors and Nets in the games he has missed.

Brooklyn pulled within five points during the final five minutes Monday, putting the Sixers in another clutch game.

Philadelphia has reached 10 clutch wins more quickly than almost any team since the statistic began being tracked in 1996-97. The 2026-27 Sixers are the second team in that span with at least 10 such victories through their first 15 games.

The other was the 1997-98 Atlanta Hawks, who had 12.

James was also on the last team before Philadelphia to open a season with at least 10 consecutive clutch wins. The Lakers started 13-0 in those games last season.

James did not play in the Sixers' latest close finish, but he has now been part of the last two teams to start at least 10-0 under those circumstances.

Before Sunday's game, Carlisle explained why he tries to limit his play calls against James.

"LeBron James is one of the reasons we try not to have play calls, 'cause he knows all of them," Carlisle said. "He's a walking NBA basketball AI machine. That's what he is."

James followed with 15 points, 11 assists and seven rebounds in the Sixers' 131-124 win.

His pass to VJ Edgecombe for a layup with 1:19 remaining gave him 12,092 career assists and moved him past Kidd for third in NBA history. Only Chris Paul and John Stockton remain ahead of him.

Noah Levick wrote more about James' milestone and the anticipation behind his passes.

When told what Carlisle had called him, James appreciated the description.

"So, yeah, Rick is great," James said. "He's one of the greatest coaches of all time, and I've had my battles with him over the years. That's super humbling coming from a guy like that."

Philadelphia has found its soundtrack for James' biggest home moments.

The Sixers have played the viral "LeBron, LeBron, LeBron" song after several of his baskets, including his late putback against the Knicks on Oct. 30.

The refrain comes from a version of Steve Lacy's "Oh Yeah?" that replaces "Come on" with James' name. The Baylor University Men's Choir performed its own rendition, helping the song spread across social media.

Lacy later joined in. During an Oct. 15 concert at Freedom Mortgage Pavilion in Camden, he performed the LeBron version as the crowd sang along.

James has noticed Philadelphia's use of it.

"I've seen it. I've heard it. It's pretty hilarious, to be honest," James said. "I heard it towards the end of a game when we were winning. I made a play, and I heard that song. I was just chuckling to myself on the inside."

#2768. 7 smart reasons to sell a stock—and my ‘golden rule’ for offloading shares, says hedge fund CEO

Nobody is born knowing anything innately about the stock market. And if you feel like you've missed the investing class, you didn't. Usually there isn't one.

So responsible people who are trying to do the responsible thing by building a portfolio for themselves and their families often find huge gaps in their knowledge and don't know where to turn.

As the co-founder and CEO of the hedge fund Metropolitan Capital Advisors and a longtime panelist on CNBC's Fast Money, I know that there's no shortage of advice when you're getting started about great investments to buy. It can feel easy and gratifying, especially when your portfolio is doing well. But then things get tougher.

One of the hardest things to do as an investor is to figure out when to sell. You buy a stock because you believe it will go up in value. But the decision to sell is rarely so straightforward. Personally, I've made just about every selling mistake. But over time, I've developed a framework for knowing when to sell — and when not to.

Selling decisions are often rooted in emotion and cognitive bias rather than logic. One major culprit: anchoring to past performance, a flawed reference point that clouds clear thinking.

Behavioral economics, particularly prospect theory, shows that we experience the pain of a loss more intensely than the joy of a gain. This leads investors to cling to losers ("just need to get back to even") and sell winners too early ("lock in the gain").

We also place too much emphasis on recent events, a phenomenon known as recency bias. If your last tech stock soared, you're more likely to chase another. If your last trade flopped, you may avoid the market altogether. And when recent trades go well, people tend to trade more — often to their detriment.

Finally, selling triggers a storm of second-guessing and rationalization. Here are my responses to common excuses:

These biases and emotional landmines are why you need a clear, disciplined plan for when to sell, including when:

When you've decided to sell for a solid reason — especially the first, second, or third outlined above — don't hesitate. Don't wait for the perfect price. Just sell.

Just as important as knowing when to sell is knowing when not to sell. Avoid emotionally driven decisions that sabotage long-term gains.

Selling well is an advanced investing skill that demands discipline, logic and self-awareness, and there's no place for ego. If you create a sound game plan, you'll be equipped to act confidently, even in the face of uncertainty. I keep a list of reasons to sell handy so I can literally look at it and keep my wits about me.

Karen Finerman is co-founder and CEO of the New York-based hedge fund Metropolitan Capital Advisors and a longtime panelist on CNBC's Fast Money. She is the author of the New York Times bestselling book, "Finerman's Rules: Secrets I'd Only Tell My Daughters About Business and Life" and the host of the podcast How She Does It. Karen is a proud member of the Wharton Board of Advisors, her alma mater, and serves on the board of The Michael J. Fox Foundation for Parkinson's Research.

#2767. Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double

Observations after Sixers beat Bucks to move to 3-0, Embiid posts triple-double originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stayed unbeaten Saturday night at Xfinity Mobile Arena, earning a 127-116 win over the Bucks and improving to 3-0 on the season.

Joel Embiid had his 10th career regular-season triple-double, tallying 28 points, 11 rebounds and 10 assists.

On his 30th birthday, Jaylen Brown posted 27 points and five assists. Tyrese Maxey had 26 points. VJ Edgecombe scored 17 and grabbed 13 boards.

The Sixers' absences on the first night of a back-to-back included LeBron James (low back/sciatica management). Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said pregame that he did not know yet who would be available for his team's matchup with the Pistons on Sunday.

Here are observations on the Sixers' victory Saturday:

Dean Wade made his Sixers debut and started in James' place. He'd missed the first two games of the regular season with a left ankle sprain.

The 3-and-D forward scored the night's first points by a sinking a corner three-pointer on the opening possession.

Embiid also had a sharp start. He moved well, made good decisions and served as the main hub of his team's offense in the early going.

The Sixers' seven-time All-Star center has often enjoyed facing Myles Turner. He entered the night with an average of 29.4 points in 18 previous games against the Bucks big man.

Embiid did a little bit of everything in the first quarter, posting eight points, four assists and four rebounds over nine minutes. After snatching an offensive board, Embiid drained a fadeaway jumper to give the Sixers a 10-1 lead.

One of Embiid's assists was quite stylish. He found Edgecombe open beyond the arc with a behind-the-back bounce pass. Edgecombe knocked down the shot and wound up going 3 for 4 from three-point range in the first quarter.

The Sixers added rookie Labaron Philon Jr. to their rotation. Jabari Walker stayed in the mix, though he didn't enter until the 7:36 mark of the second quarter.

Philon played four minutes in his NBA debut and missed his lone shot attempt, a corner three. On defense, Philon allowed a couple of blow-bys off the dribble. He generally looked like he belonged on offense, albeit without any production in his brief run.

Maxey banked in a half-court heave at the first-quarter buzzer to put the Sixers up nine points. The Sixers couldn't carry forward any momentum, however, and began the second quarter poorly.

Adem Bona had another rough stint amid a subpar start to the season. After Anfernee Simons missed a transition layup try, Ryan Rollins' three lifted the Bucks into a 40-37 lead.

The dreadful stretch for the Sixers on both sides of the ball called to mind how they'd fallen into a 29-point deficit Thursday night vs. the Cavaliers before clawing out a comeback win.

The Sixers mustered just seven points in first six minutes of the second quarter and had many stagnant possessions that concluded with contested jumpers. The team's transition defense was dismal for much of the second period. Milwaukee had the game's first 19 fast-break points.

The Sixers regained the lead late in the second quarter. Kentavious Caldwell-Pope made two three-balls in the second and nabbed three first-half steals. Embiid assisted a go-ahead, and-one Walker layup.

Brown hit a three with 11.8 seconds left in the second quarter. Unsurprisingly, his variety of shotmaking skills were useful without James in the lineup.

Even when James or Embiid sit, the Sixers should have a simple advantage in terms of volume of stars over most opponents this year. None of Brown, Embiid or Maxey had the sort of off night that would have improved the Bucks' odds of a road win. They'd all passed the 20-point mark by the end of the third quarter.

Maxey notched another final-possession bucket, this time with a more conventional make. He canned a jumper from the right elbow with 1.7 seconds left in the third to extend the Sixers' lead to 97-87.

The Bucks made a late push in the fourth quarter. Kel'el Ware's put-back, and-one layup cut the Milwaukee's deficit to 117-114.

The Sixers ultimately handled business. Embiid reached his triple-double by assisting a second-chance Brown jumper and and Caldwell-Pope soon drilled a dagger corner three. Embiid even threw down a last-minute dunk for good measure.

For the second consecutive season, the Sixers have a 3-0 start. They got to four wins last year before dropping their first game.

#2766. Houston Dash hire former NWSL Coach of the Year Seb Hines as next head coach

The Houston Dash have hired Seb Hines, formerly of the Orlando Pride, as its next head coach, the club announced Tuesday.

Hines won the 2024 NWSL Shield and Championship with the Pride, firsts for the club, and was also voted Coach of the Year that season. The Pride fired him in late August 2026, and he will be the seventh head coach in Dash history.

"Seb brings a track record of success that truly aligns with our ambitions for the Houston Dash. He understands what it takes to compete for championships in the NWSL, because he has done it before, and we are confident in his ability to maximize the potential of our roster and establish the standards necessary for sustained success," said Dash president of women's soccer Angela Hucles Mangano in a club statement.

"Our ambition is bigger than a single season or postseason appearance; we want to build a team that consistently competes at the highest level and contends for trophies. We believe Seb is the right leader to help us realize that ambition," Hucles added.

The Dash finished 12th in the league despite a strong rookie class and a bright start, winning three of their first four matches of the season. Forward Kat Rader was one goal shy of tying the record for most goals scored by a rookie in a season (10) and earned a Rookie of the Year nomination. Midfielder Linda Ullmark was named to the U.S. roster for the U-20 Women's World Cup, which took place this September in Poland. Veteran Dash goalkeeper Jane Campbell was also nominated for a Goalkeeper of the Year award this season.

In September, the Dash parted ways with former head coach Fabrice Gautrat and technical director Twila Kilgore, who was the interim head coach of the senior U.S. women's national team. Then-assistant coach Jaime Frias was promoted to interim head coach for the remainder of the Dash's season.

Hines will likely hire a new coaching staff in his new role in Houston. The two assistant coaches he worked with in Orlando have since become head coaches. Yolanda Thomas was promoted to interim head coach after Hines' departure and hired permanently last week as head coach of the Pride, becoming the first Black female head coach in NWSL history. Giles Barnes, also a former Pride assistant coach, left the club in July and leads Al Qadsiah FC as head coach in the Saudi Women's Premier League.

"Ultimately, we want to build something this city and the fans can be proud of, give them a team they are excited to watch and create sustained success for this club," Hines said in a statement. "I look forward to working closely with Angela, our ownership group and the players as we turn that shared vision into results and that work is underway."

With Hines and Thomas as managers, the NWSL will have two Black permanent head coaches for the first time in the league.

#2765. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2764. St. Louis Fed's Musalem says four more rate hikes may be needed in coming months

St. Louis Federal Reserve President Alberto Musalem indicated Tuesday that the central bank may need as many as four rate hikes in coming months, offering one of the most hawkish views yet by a Fed policymaker about the steps needed to quell inflation.

During a speech delivered in Argentina, Musalem made the case that current policy is not significantly restricting economic activity.

"The economy will likely require several interest rate increases before the spring of 2027 to achieve 2% inflation in a timely manner," he said.

Musalem said the real policy rate, which subtracts the inflation rate from the Fed's target rate, needs to be between 2%-2.5% by next spring, and estimated that the current rate is about a full point below that range. That position suggests the Fed may need to hike at each of the upcoming Fed meetings in December, January, March and April.

Musalem does not get a vote on the rate-setting Federal Open Market Committee over the period where he says that the rate increases will be needed, though he does get to present views and provide input on the "dot plot" of individual members' expectations. He will next vote in 2028.

To be sure, Musalem noted "an unlikely but still plausible scenario" in which no further hikes would be needed.

"Falling energy prices and lower shipping costs, or an increase in potential output growth driven by higher productivity growth, might ease inflation pressures such that inflation moves toward target in 18 months," he said. Another possibility is that demand might decelerate as spending on artificial intelligence slows.

However, he said the more likely scenario is that "persistent strong demand and ongoing supply pressures" combine to keep inflation above the Fed's 2% target, thus requiring further increases in the fed funds rate from its current range of 3.75%-4%.

"It would be preferable for a higher real policy rate to happen in a balanced way, driven both by lower inflation and a higher nominal policy rate," he said. "However, the nominal policy rate may need to do most of the work."

Pratt, a longtime Seahawks fan, has watched Macdonald build a winning culture in Seattle. His comments come as the team enters its Week 11 bye with a 6-4 record and two straight victories.

Pratt's praise adds another voice to the growing support for Macdonald's approach.

GettyNEW YORK, NEW YORK – OCTOBER 20: Chris Pratt attends Prime Video's "The Terminal List" Season 2 New York Premiere at Metrograph on October 20, 2026 in New York City. (Photo by Mike Coppola/Getty Images)

Pratt's connection to Seattle runs deeper than a passing interest in football.

The offense also came through despite the absence of injured starter Sam Darnold.

Lock completed 14 of 17 passes for 240 yards and two touchdowns against Las Vegas. Wide receiver Jaxon Smith-Njigba finished with 171 receiving yards.

Macdonald now has time to address the team's injuries before Seattle visits the San Francisco 49ers on November 29.

The Seahawks still have work ahead in a competitive NFC West. But Pratt's message reflects the confidence Macdonald has inspired in one of the franchise's most recognizable supporters.

#2760. Investor Steve Eisman says the AI neocloud stocks are in 'big trouble' from Nvidia fallout

Investor Steve Eisman said the so-called AI neocloud stocks will be the biggest losers from the unfolding Nvidia scandal.

Nvidia was essentially a "virtual vertical holding company and it's unwinding," said Eisman of "Big Short" fame on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "I think neoclouds are all in big trouble though because they are part of that vertical holding company and I think they're about to get ejected."

Neoclouds, which used borrowed funds to build data centers, have been among the hardest-hit stocks during the recent pullback in the artificial intelligence trade. CoreWeave and Nebius are each down more than 50% the last three months.

All eyes on Wall Street are on chipmaker Nvidia as it reports earnings on Tuesday after the bell while grappling with a potential Chinese espionage scandal. Founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday under a cloud of controversary revolving around his affair with a suspected Chinese spy. The Commerce Department has subsequently moved to close a loophole on China's remote use of data centers.

Investors are worried as scrutiny increases on Nvidia's China revenue, it may pull back on its extensive financing that supported the AI industry.

Frontier algorithm company Anthropic is expected to make its public debut on Thursday as concerns about the company's profitability potential linger amid the rapid and ongoing commodification of AI.

Eisman said he doesn't think the much-ballyhooed offering is very important anymore to the AI trade because expectations have come down.

"I don't think anybody really cares," the investor said.

Instead, Eisman is focusing on how much Democrats will restrict data center building when they take control of Congress and various governorships in January.

"I think the biggest issues by far are how much are, how much are local and state governments going to slow AI data center construction? Is it going to be 100% moratorium? That would obviously be — that would put the country into recession overnight. Or is it something like 10% to 30%?" he said.

In the event of a 10% to 30% slowdown in data center construction, Eisman said the group of companies within the AI trade that he favors the most are the hyperscalers.

"The group that makes out the best are the hyperscalers because they go cash flow positive again," he said.

Correction: Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday. An earlier version misstated the day.

#2759. James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays ‘before they actually happen'

James reaches assists milestone, keeps seeing plays 'before they actually happen' originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Tyrese Maxey admitted that he wasn't entirely ready for LeBron James' first pass of the afternoon Sunday.

Maxey cut through the paint and James' bounce pass was perfect, but the Sixers' All-NBA guard missed his layup. He's not fully accustomed yet to James' precognitive gifts.

"There's still some times when you're surprised," Maxey said after the Sixers' win over the Pacers. "… I was cutting through just to cut and create space. The ball hit me in the chest and I missed the layup. I was like, 'Ah shoot, my bad.' He was like, 'Yeah, you weren't looking.'"

James has been trying to teach his teammates that he'll spot openings they may not notice.

"I just tell my guys, 'Always pay attention to me when I've got the ball,' because I definitely see plays develop before they happen," James said.

For the most part, James' teammates adhered to his request on the latest historic day in a milestone-filled career. By setting up VJ Edgecombe's layup with 1:19 left in the fourth quarter, James moved into third place on the NBA's all-time assists list, passing Jason Kidd.

The 22-time All-Star recorded 11 assists, his most so far as a Sixer, and now sits at 12,092 in his career. Chris Paul (12,552 assists) and John Stockton (15,806) are the only two players above James.

"It's obviously super humbling," James said. "It's something that I didn't foresee in my career, but I knew assists meant a lot to me. Just the movement of the ball, being able to find my teammates, putting the ball on time and on target.

"And also just being linked with some of the greats that have ever played this game and a dear friend of mine, J-Kidd. It's really special."

James is at or near the top of many all-time leaderboards. He's first in NBA history in points, games and minutes, sixth in steals and fifth in triple-doubles.

Passing has always been a core element of his game.

"First of all, he's got the vision and the awareness," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "He's got the size to see. I think he's got some anticipation. Just a half-second before a play happens, he knows how the defense is moving. … And he throws crisp passes. Some of those are cooking out to the corner three guys, so it doesn't let the close-out get there nearly as quickly."

Whether it's a long-range rocket, a drive-and-kick dish or a cross-court feed from the post that suggests he might have eyes in the back of his head, James has always enjoyed finding a wide range of ways to help his teammates.

"For someone that talented, he's very unselfish," Maxey said. "He really prides himself on getting guys open."

As James recalled Sunday, he had exceptional passing tools long before he entered the NBA 24 seasons ago.

"Since I was a kid, I've had the ability to see plays before they actually happen," he said. "Seeing movement, seeing cracks in the defense before they were even open … it's something that's been with me since I was a kid.

"I just had that vision and it just stuck with me throughout my playing days."

#2758. Chase Home Lending CEO says he plans to 'go on offense' against non-bank lenders

A version of this article first appeared in the CNBC Property Play newsletter with Diana Olick. Property Play covers new and evolving opportunities for the real estate investor, from individuals to venture capitalists, private equity funds, family offices, institutional investors and large public companies. Sign up to receive future editions, straight to your inbox.

Mortgage rates shot higher again last week, after four straight weeks of declines. A hotter-than-expected monthly inflation report, coupled with new headlines from the Iran war, had the average rate on the 30-year fixed mortgage up 33 basis points to 7.5%, according to Mortgage News Daily.

Total mortgage demand, including both refinancing and new purchase loans, is now down just over 39% from a year ago, according to the Mortgage Bankers Association. Despite all that, Sean Grzebin, the CEO of Chase Home Lending, the nation's largest bank mortgage lender, said he's hiring and bent on increasing market share.

"For the last several years, we haven't really added a lot of salespeople," said Grzebin, who admits plainly that this is the worst market in decades. "We're going to hire a bunch of home lending advisors across the United States. We're going to focus on areas where we haven't had a lot of coverage, but there's a lot of mortgage activity and transactions taking place, and it really matches the expansion that we're having within the branch network on the consumer bank side."

Grzebin said he is looking to take back market share from non-bank lenders, who flourished after the financial crisis, when banks were cleaning up from thousands of failed home loans. In August, JPMorganChase announced a 40% increase in home lending capital through 2035 to $750 billion. It will also hire 850 new lending agents.

"I think the non-banks are all very formidable competitors. I think they've had an advantage while the banks were distracted through several decades," he said. "I think now you're starting to see the resurgence of the banks, and I think a lot of banks are actually going to grow their mortgage business through this."

He said he expects to see continued consolidation in the mortgage market, especially as artificial intelligence penetrates the origination part of the business. Chase is investing heavily in AI for underwriting, he said.

"We're hiring mainly in our sales functions because we think we can grow by adding people that have relationships with existing customers today, as well as them taking advantage of our branch network to help grow their business on one side," explained Grzebin. "And we think we can achieve all this growth without adding any incremental headcount in our operation because of the streamlining of the process that's happening with AI."

Chase just ranked highest in mortgage origination satisfaction in J.D. Power's annual U.S. Mortgage Origination Satisfaction Study. While overall satisfaction was unchanged from 2025, just 58% of borrowers said they receive same-day responses from their lender when they ask questions, down from 62% a year ago. Fewer borrowers also say they have a main point of contact, down to 88% from 91% a year ago.

"Borrowers appreciate being able to handle more of the mortgage process online, but they still want someone they can turn to when they have questions," said Bruce Gehrke, senior director of wealth and lending intelligence at J.D. Power in a release. "J.D. Power is seeing less recognition of updates and guidance that are important to keeping borrowers informed. Lenders have an opportunity to make the process more convenient while ensuring borrowers feel supported, especially as they approach closing."

That appears to be what Chase is leaning into as so much of the process now moves to agentic AI. Grzebin said they focused first on the closing process, which customers said was most frustrating. Then they moved to digital tools.

"There's a lot of opportunity there that we're going to be able to take advantage of in the next 12 to 24 months, just making it easier for customers to do more self-service through AI capabilities that we're going to also roll out over the next couple quarters, and so we are invested heavily in AI," he said. "We're going to see some real efficiency from it, and then on the sales side, adding salespeople, but then also figuring out a way to make them more productive."

Servicing is also becoming a bigger factor again, as mortgage delinquencies slowly rise, especially in the FHA space. These low-down-payment loans generally carry higher risk, and as rates stay stubbornly high, those risks are starting to emerge. Chase does do FHA lending, but it is a smaller part of its business.

"I think others are going to have other problems to deal with, particularly if there's any unemployment, and we're just going to be in good shape for that entire cycle. So we look at it as a chance to really go on offense," he said, specifically in FHA lending.

"The non-banks stepped into it and served that market, and that business is being challenged right now, and so we're trying to get back into it," Grzebin said. "We'd love to get back into it. We're priced competitively to get back into it."

#2757. Dodgers protect four prospects from Rule 5 draft, including top pitcher snatched from Yankees

There might not be a Rule 5 draft in Major League Baseball this winter, with an upcoming lockout threatening to cancel the annual event.

But the Dodgers didn't take any chances on Tuesday, still electing to protect four minor-league prospects who would've been Rule 5 eligible.

In a series of roster moves Tuesday, the Dodgers added pitchers Christian Zazueta, Jackson Ferris, Adam Serwinoski and Luke Fox to their 40-man roster, while designating for assignment pitcher Jake Cousins and recent waiver claim infielder Christian Cairo.

The Dodgers' reigning Minor League Pitcher of the Year Christian Zazueta is continuing his rise through the franchise's minor-league system. MLB Photos via Getty Images

The moves ensure none of the four young pitchers will be subject to a possible Rule 5 draft — in which non-40-man players who have accrued a certain amount of time in their organizations become available for other teams to "draft" and add to their own roster — while also subjecting Cousins (a veteran reliever recovering from Tommy John surgery) and Cairo (who was claimed from the Mets last week) to waivers if they are to remain in the organization.

The last time MLB experienced an offseason lockout, during the 2021-22 offseason, the Rule 5 draft (which is usually held during the winter meetings in December) was canceled and not rescheduled.

It's possible that could be the case again this year, with the league's current CBA set to expire on Dec. 1. But last week, general manager Brandon Gomes said the club would operate as if things were "business as usual," leading the Dodgers to protect their four prospects ahead of a Tuesday afternoon deadline.

Brandon Gomes, general manager of the Los Angeles Dodgers, speaks with media during the MLB general manager meetings in Las Vegas, Tuesday, Nov. 10, 2026. AP Photo/John Locher

The most obvious choice was Zazueta, a 22-year-old right-handed starter whom the Dodgers acquired for reliever Caleb Ferguson from the Yankees three years ago in what has become a steal of a transaction.

A little-known talent at that time, Zazueta has since blossomed into one of the most promising young arms in baseball. He is ranked as the top pitcher (and fifth overall player) in the Dodgers' farm system. He is the No. 33 overall prospect in the sport, according to MLB Pipeline. And he is coming off a strong 2026 campaign in which he posted a 3.44 ERA in his first stint in Double-A and won the club's Minor League Pitcher of the Year award for a second straight season.

Serwinoski and Ferris, two other trade acquisitions in recent years, were also added to the 40-man roster as expected.

Ferris has been on a bit of a roller coaster since joining the organization in the Michael Busch trade from the Cubs, along with since-traded outfielder Zyhir Hope (who was a key part of the deadline blockbuster for Tarik Skubal this past summer). The 22-year-old had impressive stints in Double-A in 2024 and 2025, but then saw his ERA balloon over 7.00 during his first four months in Triple-A this year.

Former Yankees pitcher Jake Cousins, posing for a photo at Steinbrenner Field during spring training in 2025, was DFA'd by the Dodgers as part of a roster shuffle on Tuesday. Charles Wenzelberg / New York Post

From Aug. 16 on, however, the left-hander posted a 2.08 ERA over his final seven appearances (39 innings), showing enough promise to reestablish himself as a future piece for the Dodgers — potentially as early as next year.

Serwinoski, another left-hander, has had his own ups and downs (including a 5.31 ERA in Double-A this year) but is still seen as a projectable big-league southpaw further down the line.

Fox was the lone homegrown player of the group, drafted by the Dodgers out of Duke as a 17th-rounder in 2023. He was recovering from Tommy John surgery at the time but has pitched to a 2.91 ERA since his return in 2024, finishing this past season in Double-A.

Cousins, a 32-year-old reliever who hasn't appeared in the big leagues since 2024 with the Yankees because of injuries, was the most notable roster cut, after spending all of last year with the team rehabbing from a 2025 Tommy John procedure.

Cairo, a 25-year-old utilityman who has yet to make his MLB debut, was only acquired in a waiver claim last week from the Mets, who had DFA'd him in their own roster crunch.

#2756. Lisa Cook lawyer signals Fed governor won't attend Trump committee's hearing

Federal Reserve Governor Lisa Cook's attorney on Tuesday gave the strongest signal yet that his client will not attend a hearing at the White House with a "committee of inquiry" investigating mortgage fraud allegations against Cook.

The committee established last month by President Donald Trump, who has previously tried to remove Cook from the central bank's board, is pretextual and poses a threat to the Fed, attorney Abbe Lowell wrote in a letter to presidential counsel Will Scharf.

"Governor Cook has no legal obligation to participate in an unfair hearing — and indeed, has a duty to the Federal Reserve to decline to participate in any proceeding that would undermine the institution's unique status and independence," Lowell wrote in the letter, which is dated Monday and was made public Tuesday.

The latest correspondence came nearly two weeks after the White House committee delayed its proposed hearing with Cook to Dec. 14 from Nov. 5. Lowell said that any hearing with Cook could not take place until at least December because of conflicts with her Fed duties.

While Lowell had raised strong concerns from the outset about the legitimacy of the committee, he initially said Cook "welcomes the opportunity to present the facts so she can clear her name."

But he has continued to cast doubts on both the committee's purpose and its composition of three executive branch officials, including National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett.

Lowell's tone sounded more hostile than ever in this week's letter, the first known to be sent by either party in the matter since the Republican Party's devastating losses in the midterm elections on Nov. 3.

He accused Scharf and the committee of seeking "only to manufacture 'cause' for a predetermined removal outcome."

Lowell highlighted two top concerns. First, he asserted that Hassett "is not competent to serve on any factfinding committee" because his prior comments about Cook "demonstrate that he has prejudged the issue."

Second, Lowell called it "fundamentally unfair" that the committee is demanding Cook hand over documents "immediately," while pledging to provide the governor with its own records just seven days before the hearing.

"Your and the Committee's failure to address these legitimate issues, over multiple weeks of back-and-forth correspondence, reinforces my prior observation: President Trump's decision to fire Governor Cook has already been made," Lowell wrote.

"Consequently, this Committee process appears to be a façade designed to obscure a predetermined decision to fire Governor Cook or to unreasonably manufacture a supposed 'adverse inference' if she does not participate in a process that does not comport with the requirements of a fair hearing," the lawyer wrote.

Lowell told Scharf that Cook would still be prepared to appear for a hearing if the White House "implements fair procedures." But the "ultimatums" Scharf gave in his last reply in early November "do not provide a productive path forward to enabling that participation," Lowell wrote.

The White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on Lowell's letter.

Trump, who frequently urges the Fed to lower interest rates, has long sought to fire Cook, an appointee of former President Joe Biden.

Cook, the first Black woman to serve on the Fed's Board of Governors, last voted to cut rates in December 2025, siding with the majority. The Fed unanimously voted to raise rates after its meeting in September.

Trump officials sent a criminal referral last year alleging Cook may have committed mortgage fraud by falsely claiming different properties as her primary residence in order to get more favorable terms. Cook adamantly denied wrongdoing, and, after Trump tried to fire her in August 2025, filed a lawsuit to block her removal.

The Supreme Court in June prevented Trump from firing Cook, but gave him a path to try again if additional legal steps were taken. In August, the White House told Cook it was reviving its efforts to fire her on mortgage-related grounds. In October, Trump signed a presidential memo establishing the committee of inquiry to investigate allegations that Cook lied on records related to "one or more mortgage instruments."

#2755. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2754. Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return

Paul George flips the script — and the bird — in Philadelphia return originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

PG-13 was rated R in his return to Philadelphia.

The middle finger Paul George directed toward one baseline fan only reinforced that rating.

Tuesday night was expected to revolve around Jaylen Brown and Jayson Tatum sharing an NBA court as opponents for the first time. Instead, the other headliner in the summer's blockbuster trade stole the show.

George scored a game-high 36 points in Boston's 123-101 win over the Sixers. He shot 14 for 22 from the floor, made six of his 11 three-point attempts and scored 14 consecutive Boston points in the fourth quarter.

The game was tied at 79 with 3:19 remaining in the third quarter. Boston outscored Philadelphia 44-22 from that point forward.

The Celtics went nine minutes and 20 seconds without making a two-point field goal during the second half. Instead, they made nine consecutive field goals from three, the second-longest such streak by a Sixers opponent over the last 30 seasons.

Payton Pritchard opened the fourth with consecutive threes. George followed with eight straight points, helping the Celtics begin the quarter on a 14-0 run that turned a competitive game into a rout.

George's return carried a complicated history. His two seasons with the Sixers were repeatedly interrupted by injuries, but he also played a significant role in their most memorable playoff run in years.

Last spring, George helped the Sixers overcome a 3-1 deficit to eliminate Boston in seven games. He averaged 17.4 points and shot 55 percent from three during the series.

Tyrese Maxey was happy to see his former teammate Tuesday, even if he disliked what followed.

"It was good to see Paul," Maxey said. "I love him, love his family. … He gave us everything he had while he was here. We know what he did in the playoffs last year. Great person, great family, and we'll see him again."

Maxey then added one important qualifier.

George instead scored from nearly everywhere.

"He had some open threes. He had some pull-up threes. He had some middies," Maxey said. "He played within the game, too. That's what Paul does. He plays within the game. He takes what the defense gives him, and we gave him too many open looks tonight.

"PG, we know what PG does. We shouldn't even be allowing him to get up 11 threes, and that's on us."

Nick Nurse watched George attack the Sixers in several ways.

"He really had it going," Nurse said. "He'd post up, and-one, fallaways. He went down the lane, high archer off the glass, and-one. He scored in about every way he could tonight."

George heard boos during his return and said the arena carried the atmosphere, though not quite the intensity, of a playoff game.

"This building can be very electric as well," George said. "It was fun. It was fun to play in this environment."

One interaction extended beyond the usual reception for a returning player.

During a stoppage, the ball rolled toward a fan seated along the baseline. George said the fan looked directly at him before dropping the ball instead of returning it.

"There was a fan being an a–," George said. "The ball rolled to him. He looked me dead in my eyes, dropped the ball on the ground as if I'm a dog to go fetch or go get it, instead of passing the ball to me like a normal fan would do.

"And so, yeah, then I rolled him a middle finger in return."

#2752. Warriors Rookie Yaxel Lendeborg’s Trash Talk Gets Honest Response From Former Coach Dusty May

SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA - NOVEMBER 09: Yaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors talks to Derik Queen #22 of the New Orleans Pelicans before they both received a technical foul in the fourth quarter at Chase Center on November 09, 2026 in San Francisco, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Lachlan Cunningham/Getty Images) Getty

Golden State Warriors fans found out who Yaxel Lendeborg was in his very first NBA game. The rookie forward went back and forth with Lakers star Luka Doncic in a 127-105 loss, picked up a technical foul, and later told reporters he had lost respect for him.

That night has followed the No. 11 pick ever since. Now, with the Warriors set to visit the Dallas Mavericks on Wednesday, the man who coached him to a national title has been asked about it.

Mavericks head coach Dusty May had Lendeborg at Michigan last season. Mike Curtis of The Dallas Morning News shared a video on X in which May was asked whether he saw the same trash talk in college.

"Oh yeah, yeah. We saw it a lot," May said.

May also saw the draft from the inside. Dallas passed on Lendeborg at No. 9 in June, and Golden State took him two picks later.

"During the draft process, some people really appreciated it, but some were probably a little fearful of how transparent Yaxel is," May said. "There's no fluff, there's no coach speak. He's been media trained, but he just decides to be authentically who he is."

"You're never going to wonder where you stand with Yax. You're never going to wonder what he's thinking because he's going to let you know," May added. "That's what makes him someone that everyone enjoys being around."

Warriors head coach Steve Kerr has taken the same view. After the Doncic exchange, he said he loved that his rookie did not back down, but told him to back up his words.

GettyYaxel Lendeborg #1 of the Golden State Warriors talks to Derik Queen #22 of the New Orleans Pelicans

For most of the season, Lendeborg has done that, and May has noticed. Asked if knowing his game makes him easier to plan for, he said no.

"We had to beg him to shoot threes last year and finally he did," May said. "Now he's shooting more threes, he's creating off the bounce, he's shooting more threes off the dribble."

"He's very proud of his progression. And they've done a nice job with how they're using him as well," May added.

The numbers show that. Lendeborg is averaging more than 20 points and seven rebounds a game, and he was No. 1 on NBA.com's rookie ladder last week.

He also dropped 37 points on the LA Clippers on Nov. 1, the most by a Warriors rookie since Stephen Curry in 2010.

The last week has been harder. Lendeborg scored three points on 1-of-13 shooting in a 123-85 loss to the San Antonio Spurs, then put up 28 in Monday's loss to the New Orleans Pelicans. The Warriors are 8-6 and have lost three straight after an eight-game win streak.

Wednesday in Dallas is the next chance to stop that slide. It is also Lendeborg's first regular-season game against his old coach.
